Ghapter One: Introduction
his game is probably different from anything you've ever played. In some ways, it's not reallya game at all.There is no board, there are nodice, and there is no set way of wmnmg. Oblivion 1s more concerned with stories than rules. ln face, chis game has more in common with childhood games than with Trivial Pursuit or Mo­
,   nopoly.Thisgameallows you to confront the darkness inside usall;
it enables you co assume the role of a ghost caught between the need cocling to the living and the necessity of movingon.
If you have never played in a live-action roleplaying game, be prepared for a unique experience. Even if you've been playingcable-cop roleplaying games for years, you will find that live-action gaming feels different. The game doesn't exist solely m your mind any more; now it comes to life around you. The rush of adrenaline and the feeling of"being" your character is much more intense. We have a name for thisstyle of game. We call it Mind's Eye Theatre.
The Artof Storytelling
Before you become engrossed in the rules sections, remember that Oblivion is flrsc and foremost a storytelling game. A good story should cake precedence over a rule. Ifeveryone involved in a dispute can agree on the outcome chat concraJicts the rules of this book, then ignore the rules. Rules are a safety net for when players can't
decide what should happen in a siniac1on, and a way to get everyone ro agree on what should happen. They should not be the be-all and end-all of your game, ocher-
wise the spontanciry and 1mprovisatton char makes hve,acuon roleplaymg so special c:in get lost.
ManLanJ ha, l>een tdlmg ,tone, for as long as we've had the ('O\\Cr of speech. Smee the earliest days of talc, cold by cavemen around their fires, we have created mcreJ1bly chven-e rypes of media to tell our Mories. Booh, radio, <lnima, celev1sion and even computer networks have camed our tales to others, and enabled us to hear
,cones we might ne\•er have he-ard otherw1-c, One of the newest metho,hof storytelling
1s that of live-acuon rolcplaymg, which grew out of the first table-top roleplaying g,1mes of the '70 .Of the many lhrecuons which gammg- has raken smce rhen, one of the mosr important has been rhe emphasis on character nnd story, which has led to
the creatmn of what we c1II '\toryceller" gnmes. The book you hold m your hands 1\ an attempt to explore the houndarie:. nfl1ve acnon and ,ror,:rellmg m gaming.
In recent years many people have given up their Jc,1re co tell stories, content only to !men co rhe stones rold by others. Those stories come flooding m from rhe TV, the Internet and a thousand other -<)urces, and some people believe they don't need to make their own stories any more By jvining a 1,.-ame of Oblivion, by reading this book, you are agreeing to help create a new ,tory, one m which you are both actor and co-author. It's a big step to take, but the end result can be incredibly re­ warding. To be able to look hack at a g.imc where you were pivotal in detenninmg what actually happened can give a feelin2 of accomph,hmcm that no movie or tele­ v1,ion show can match.

T e live-Action Gamingparaaigm
Table-top roleplaym2, while 1c 101ru. ix•ople in creating a story, snll lcaves mo,c of the story under the control of one person. In this oort of semng, the Sror;rdler has co dt.>SCribe the ennre world and ilny addmonal characters thar the rest of the gaming group encounters. The Storyteller h;is coral control of nearly e,·erything in the worlJ and cre• ates most of the ,cory, with the players conmhuting only their inv1dual chilracters' part,.
Live-acnon gaming, on the other hand, takes much of the conrr,,I of the serrmg and places 1t m the hands of the playe"'· When a hundred or more people are cellinp a ,rory at once, the Srorytelltr does not have to describe rhe ennrc world. The Stn­ ryreller creates ideas and goals and descrihcs rhe world m termsof rulci, and descrip­ uons to players. The players then act out their characters and create the story with only mm1mal mpuc from the Storyteller. With any luck, the story will unravel it\Clf, from the rightly knoned pu: le the Scoryceller miually prc,cnts to the full flowcrinl! of plot, exclusively through rhc actions and insights of the players.
Another ma1or shift that live-acunn ir,1ming hrings ro the arr of storytelling involves e apmg the lim1t,1t1oru, of the medium. The 1,.r.ime b no longcr limited to
Chapter One: introauction
words and 1maginaticm. Costuming, prop and other visual cues can be an integral pan of a live game. Instead of a Storyteller describing rwo people talking across the room, the players actually see them stanJing in the comer whispering. A long,dimly lir corridor covered in a threadbare carpet can be seen (and if necessary avoided) rather than just described. A moonlit courtyard scoured by a wind thar whips cloaks and skirtsaround bodies - living things that can be experienced by the flesh, rather than just the imagination.
oleplaying
When you play Oblivion, you cake on the persona of a wraith.This isa charac• ter you invent and then roleplay over the course of a storyand perhaps a chronicle (a seriesof connected stones).The life of your character i m your hands, for you decide whac your character doesand say . For the purposes of the game, you are your charac­ ter. You decide what risks to accept or decline. Everything you say or do has an effect on the world.
During the game, youshould alwaysspeak as your character. Unless you're talk­ ing to a Narrator or Storyteller, whatever you say is what your character says. Be­ cause most of what a Mind's Eye Theatre player perceives will depend on the char­ acters around him, players must be vivid and expressive. The characters direct the plot, but at the same time, the eventsof the game guide and develop the characters, helping them to weave the seamless tapestry of a story.
To an extent, as a player in a storytelling game, you have a responsibility be­ yondsimply portraying your character. Sometimes players need to consider the story as a whole rather than just their individual goals and satisfaction, and often players should make sure that other players enjoy the game. In the end, you should make sure that your actions do not completely ruin the story, for the story is as important as the characters.

Characters for Oblivion are easy to create. Ir cakes about 10 minutes to work out a basic personality and characteristics. However, chat's just the beginning. The process of creating your character will never really end, because she will always be learning and experiencing new things in every game you play, buc even before your first gamestarts, it takessome effort co turn rhecollection ofT raits and statistics into a living, breathing (so ro speak) whole. Remember, the character you create is the person you're going to be for at least a night, and probably longer. The idea is to create for yourself a role that different enough from your real persona to be enjoy­ able to play, but with enough of you in it for you to be comfortable. Both statistics (called Traits) and a believable combination of story and personalicy are necessary co create a character who is enjoyable co play. When Frankenstein created his monster, the cask of sewing cogether the available parts was relatively easy, if not always thrill­ mg. Providing the final spark of life was difficult, bur much more exciting.
Cast
Most of rhe players involved in any given game of Oblivion will create and roleplay characters. Players are members of the case, and their characters are central co the story. Being a cast member does not demand as much responsibility as being

As a cast member, you rry to do things that allow your character ro "succeed,'' achieving goals or helping others to do so within the context of the story. Making sure thar your goals, strategies and schemes are carried out, and thar 1::vents work to your advantage while all of che ocher players are doing the samt> thmg creates the tension thar drive thegame's story.
Narrators
Any story requires at least one person to serve as its narrator. In Mind's Eye Theatre, Namnor is the name given to a person wh<) runs the game as a whole rather than just her md1vidual character.The impanial judges who run d,e game, Narrators describe scenes and events that cannot bestaged, adJudicate rules, and occasionally play the roles of antagonists. Generally, enlist mgone Narrator for every six players is a good ratio. That ratio can be adjusted, as the best number of Narrators for your game usually depends upon how much experience the people in your game have.
Narrators also takecare of what happens when a character decides to interact with something m the game other thananother character. Ifa character needs cosne-ak pasta watchful Cenrurion or access Jata from a computer in the landsof the living, the Narra­ tor plays the Centurion and determines how difficult the data is to retrieve. Anything a player's 1magmacion can con.1ure, a Narracor may be called upon to recreate.
Storyteller
The Storyceller is the ultimate authority and final Judge m Oblivion. The Sto­ ryccller creates the clementbof the btory that isbeing cold, and 1s in charge of making sun! that the srory unfolds well. Bemg the Storyteller is a demanding job, but it can be the most rewarding of all,foe the Storyteller is the weaver of the tapestry that all of the ocher playerb detail.

ules
Although the main idea of thisgame 1s co roleplayand cellstories, Oblivion still concams rules.They are needed to resolve what happens when a character doessome­ l.l,ing that the player cannot do {like walking through a wall or making portraits bleed) or does nor want to do (like engaging in combar wuh another character).
Rules are necessary to settle arguments - otherwise the game would bog down in the schoolyard dynamics of "Bang, bang, you're dead!" "You missed!" - and they add a vital measure of consistency to events in your world. Furthermore, the rules of Mind's Eye Theatre mgeneral and Oblivion in particular are designed co fit smoothly into gameplay and facilitate it, rather than delay it. A simple contest (see the Lexi• con) enables quick and easy resolution of disputes or conflico;, d1en it's back co roleplaying.
While rules are important, they are part of the game, not the game itself. The rule should serve to make things as fair as possible for all players, making cercam char the world of your game functions the same way for all of your players. They should not serve co limit roleplaying or mechanize character interactions, norshould they l;,e consulted like a sacred text at every opportunity. After all, the characters don't know rhe "rules"of their existence. TheyJust exist. Every time a rule isquoted, every rime a rulebook is opened, the illusion of the game is shattered.
Winnersand losers
There is no real way co ''win" a game when you play Oblivion. Your character can do well or poorly, bur there are no concrete conditions that grant the laurels of victory. Your character possesses motivatio.ns, ambitions and goals that helpyou de­ cide what her aspirations are in a story, but there is no poinc ac which your character will reach some sore of ultimate m-play pinnacle. Rather, there will always be new goals, challenges and antagonists to make your character's afterlife incerestmg.
Ultimately, the goal of Mind's Eye Theatre isco immerse yourself in the story and have fundoing ir. If you have fun playmg, then you cermmly cannotconsider yourself co

props
In order for others to visualize your character, you might choose to wear a cos­ tume and employ props. You must not use anything char can harm you or another cast member (seeabove).Guns,swords, garottes, motorcycle chains, asegais, nunchuks and other real or imitation weapons are strictly forbidden. The Mind's Eye Theatre system uses a bitlding system in place of any actual physical combat. A player with a weapon might be tempted co hit someone with it or wave it about in a threatening manner. Actual physical danger is never an element in the game.

Cool costumes, jewelry, makeup and the like are strongly encouraged. Some people get heavily intocostuming when playing the game,and other players appreci­ ate the effort involved in creating a ghostly appearance. Costuming, ranging from period clothing ro dangling chains to masks, is alsoa good way co help create mooJ in both yourself and other players. A player should bring whatever she needs to the Mind's Eye Theatre event to evoke the persona of her character.
C ronicle
Hopefully, you'll wane to play Oblivion more than once. Each time you play, you may choose to create a new character for a brand-new story, or you can play the same character for evening after evening. Your persona can develop like a character ma novel instead of disappearing after one brief moment in the spotlight. By playing man extended chronicle - a whole series of stories- with the samecharacter, you can help your character to develop and grow, gaining new allies, status, influence and power. There's also the possibility that your character might spiral down co the Void itself, seeking ultimate annihilation of the self. Both possibilities offer the po­ tential for spectacular journeys and superb ruleplaymg experiences.
Tne WorldofDarkness
The world of Oblivion ts a dark reflection of our own. Here, beautyandcorruption srand our in greater contrast; everything is monolithic, majestic and twisted. Though faith and human spirit remainstrong,greed and dispatr seem strongerstill.The Gothic­ Punk world that Oblivion cakes place in is a darker, more violent version of our own. "Punk" is reflected in the rejection of society by many young people, who demonstrate their rebelhon with everything from unconventional dress to violence ag-ainst "the Sys­ tem."Trapped ma cold and bleak world, they reject a society that will not accept them and struggle in the hope of something bener down the road.
Bur moreso than in Oblivion's companion games Masquerade and Apocalypse, Oblhion concentrates on the Gothic part of the equation, Think back co the Gothic noveb you've read and penod films you've seen. Whether the settmg i,, a darkly forbid­ ding city of glassand steel or a crumbling castle, there is always the sense of an era chat now exists only m memory, of a time and place unlike our own. This sense of what was
18
once new and shming covered in cobwebs and grime is present from the nightmarish Gotham Cityof the Bacman filmsto DraculascrumblingCarfux Abbey to even the vaguely art decolandscapes of the Giger-Gibson futures.What wasonce elegant or avant-gaide is now faded, and the fading colorsand crumbling fa des lllSplre both terror and pity.
Another pan of the Gothic ambiance is the senseof isolation. Whether the charac­ tersare alone on the Yorkshire moorsor in the midst of a Manhattan cocktail party, they will never again be a pan of the world of the living. Consider the mystery novels you've read, in which somehow the telephone wires are cut, the carsdisabled, and the charac­ ters crapped together, isolated from any source of help. This can be a terrifying situation for most modem people, whoare used to having the entire worldonly a phone callaway. But just as this isolation invokes an atmosphere of desperation and terror, it also forces the characters to realize that theycan only rely uponeachother, and that they alone will have to take responsibility for their fates. For many of the Restless, this is a difficult but absolutely essential lesson to learn upon crossing the Shroud.

Of course, there are many among the Resdess who would try to forget all of chis. They run with the living, losing themselves in the overwhelming onslaught of night­ clubs, with their myriad flashing lights and booming basslmes. The Restless come to these places to forget, just as the Quick do. Two hundred people are packed onto a dance floor, each trying to escape, to lose themselves in light,sound and movement. Yet even there, there is little or no contact between people. They are asalone on the dance floor as they would be were they adrift on a desolate ocean.
Both of these typesof locations, the haunted mansion or the ultra-modem club, can be appropriate for a game of Oblivion. Your choice of location will effect the mood and atmosphere of the game greatly, so choose wisely and be prepared to stand by your choice. After all, your characters are going to be spending eternity there.
itCan't in All theTime
Beyond all of this, there must always be a sense of hope. Anyone who is over­ come by the senseof decay and despair of the Shadowlands becomes easy prey for her Shadow, and will probably spiral down to the Void in no time at all.Wraiths survive in this bleak world because of their Passions, their overwhelmmg emotional needs that give them reason cogo on existing. For many wraiths, it isonly after their deaths that they find a reason to live.
While a wraith's existence is often gnm and it is sometimes hard to find area­ soncostruggle on, that is just what each character mustdo. Each soul lost coOblivion is a tragedy, but each soul that resists Oblivion's cold call can make it a little bit easier for everyone else to resist as welI.
Geograpny
he realm of the dead, also known as the Underworld, is made upof three main parts. The first one a new wraith will encoun­ ter is known among the Restless as rhe Shadowlands. A gnm reflection of the world of the Quick, the Shadowlands are stained withdecay and the perfume of death infuses everything. This is where most Oblivion games will take place, for it is
•===============-
only m the Shadowlands that wraiths can remain in a semblance of the real world.
The Shadowlands are separated from the lands of the living (or Skinlancb) by a metaphysical barrier called the Shroud.
Another area of the Underworld that wraiths may encounter is the Tempest. An eternal storm that rages just below the surface of the Shadowlands' reality, it is reachable through pits known as Nthils, or by use of the Argos Arcanos. The Tem­ pest is used to travel quickly from one point to another in the Shadowlands, without craversmg the distance between. In most games of Oblivion, the Tempest will prob­ ably be used chiefly as a travel realm, rather than as an actual story setting.
Deep within the Tempest is the thtrd sort of temtory known in the Under­ world. It is here that we find Stygia, home of the Hierarchy and capital of Charon's empire. Further into the Tempe,t are the i,lands known as the Far Shores, as well as the other Deadlandsor Dark Kingdoms. Many wraiths see the FarShoresas placesof peace and tranquility, but official Hierarchy policy is chat they are more Purgatory than Paradise.

Distant from Srygia and the FarShoresare the various other Dark Kingdoms, co which the Dead from many lands pass on. Like Stygia, each of these lands has a sy tem for classifying and dealing with the many souls that pass through its gares, but few from the West have journeyed to these lands and returned.
While tales of Srygia and her sister kingdoms across the Sea of Shadows abound, few wraiths in rhe Shadowlands haveseen these places frrst-hand. Noconly is the jour­ ney dangerous, even to the dead, but most wraiths are tied to the landsof the living by what are called Fetters. items, places or people of great importance to the wraith in life, they anchor a Restless against Oblivion's relentless pull but can also hinder her from traveling far from them. Wraiths who lose all of their Fetters cannot return to the Shadowlands, but must dwell m Stygia and the Tempest; wraiths with Fettersoften fmd their movement and actions impeded if they travel too fur from chem.
Another reason few travelers journey far m the Underworld is surely the fact that the Tempest is without a doubt the most dangerous and terrifying "place" any wraith will ever know. Navigation is difficult for even the most seasoned wraith, given the ever-shifting scenery and murky quality of the Tempest. The Tempest is also home co a great many Spectres, who search its cloudy depths for unsuspecting wraiths to capture and either descroy or convert to the worship of Oblivion.
The DarkSicte
Every wraith's dark side has a voice and a personality, and it will pur the twoco the best use it can imagine. Known as the Shadow, chis side of the wraith's persona seeks nothing more than to drag the wraith down to Oblivion. Of course, each Shadow is different and has unique motivations, drives and tastes. Shadows will even sometimes help their ocher halves, making their mcv1rable betrayals all the more shocking.

A new existence calls for new terms with which co describe it. Below are some of the phrases and names used to define Underworld exiscence.
Abilities: The measure of a wraith's expertise in specific fields. Measured in Traits. Agency, The: Slang for the bureaucracy of the Hierarchy.

affect the living and the dead. Plural is Arcanoi.

Artifact: An object in the Underworld that has unusual powers of some sort. Attributes: The measure of a wraith's basic Physical, Social and Mental statistics. Measured in Traits.
Bidding: Part of the mechanism of challenges. The risking of Traits in order to win a challenge.

Boojum: A nasry critter, usually one of the unidentified denizens of the Tempest. Byway: A stable path chrough the Tempest.

his chapter describes in detail the world of Wraith. TheUnderworld
he lands of the dead, consisting of the Far Shores, the Tempest (and all things within it),Scygia and theShadowlands arecollec­ tively known as the Underworld. Technically the other so-called Dark Kingdoms, those afterlives populated by the dead of Asia, Africa, India and other placesarealso partof the Underworld, but as they have little codowith the night-to-nightexistence of most Stygian wraiths, they tend co fall under the heading of "out of sight, out of mind." When a Western gho:;t refers to the Under­
world, he generally doesn't mean the Chinese or Polynesian Deadlands.
oblivion
The passive, patient aspect of destruction, Oblivion isa part of the naturnl order of things. le waits for all things when they break down; everything must die eventu-
ally, m order to make way for new things to be born. When kept in check by rhe forces of creation, Obl1v1on is an eswnw1l part of the cycle of death and rebirth. Mindless and eternal, Oblivion has always waned to swallow tho,e souls no longer Fettered to the Skinland, but not ready for Transcendence.
These days, however, 1r would seem chat Oblivion rages unchecked. Its pull strengthens daily as its appeme forsouls increases. Its pre:.ence mars the Underworld and rhe Skinlands to an unprecedented degree, and its tainted touch 1s everywhere. In the form of$pectre5 1t has more and more servants among the dead, and they ride more and mme frequent Maelstroms to make war upon the Restless. Simply pur, Oblivion is gmwing and no one is sure why. Most wraiths resolve to fight the en• croaching corrupnon; too many surrender to 1t.
Supposed!} there will come a day when Oblivion wallows everything.Quaintly known as Doomsday, this day is dreaded by all \Vl'31ths and eagerly anuc1pated by all Spectres. With Doomsday comes the end, so they say, of everything, both for the living and the dead.
Souls:BuildingBlocbof the Underworld
The economy and :;ociety of the Underworld are boch built on souls. Most ob­ jects m the Underworld are made of memories; the remainder are compo:,ed of smelted or reshaped -.ouk Currency consists ot coinsforged from souls; these oboli are said to moan in the hands of chose who possess chem.
As souls are the basbof cheeconomy, they are regarded less as individuals than as resources.Slavery is a booming busine intheShadowlands, ba ed not on color or creed, but on the simple proposmon chat the strong ensla\'e the weak because they can. It 1s an inhumane ,ystem, and a homfying one. Trading inSouls
The vast maiority of souls avoid the Underworld entirely. Swallowed immedi­ ately by Oblivion or lifted into Transcendence, they avoid the half-existence of
\Haiths. For tho,e who <lo become wraiths, however, their fellow Restless po,e a threat almost ai. great as Ohlivion.
Objects in the Underworld are of two kinds: memories and souls. Relics, objects that cross over from the Skinlands, are in essence created by the emotional energy invested m chem by the living; a much-loved ceddy bear or a single sock endlessly cursed over will both reappear in relicform when thrown out or destroyed. However, even w1rh the best efforts of certain wraiths to pick and choose what comes over the Shroud, the selection is still haphazard, and notably slanted toward non-essential items. For example, relicgunsarecommon, butrelic bullets are not. Relicdollsabound, but relic girders or bulldozers are lamentably scarce. ln addition, most relics exist as solid-state objects, duplicating form but not function unless invested with Pathos. Arcifacr.s, objects invested with special powers, sometimes arrive directly from the Skinlands, but infrequently. Thus, there is a need for another source of inanimate objects, one chat provides more pliable and versatile roatenals. In other words,souls.
If a new Enfant is unlucky, he'll end up being Reaped by a slaver. These Har­ vesters roam the Shadowlands, collecting wraiths who are too weak, disoriented or foolish to defend themselves. Wraiths who are enslaved (the technical term is en­ thralled) will find themselves shackled with chains forged from Stygian sLeel, and herded toward the nearest Necropolis to be sold or smelted down. A fortunate few aresimply sold intoan eternity ofservitude asThralls; the rest become raw materials in che burgeoning Stygian economy.
Just about anything can be forged from souls: girders, chains, weapons, furni­ ture, ecc. Those objects chat require a more delicate touch duringcreation are often created by wraiths skilled in the Arcru1os Moliate, who can reshape Corpus with a thought and a touch. Even the coins of rhe Underworld are made from souls, with each obolus consisting of one soul, hammered intoa press.
Of course, not every soul isconsigned to the forges. A minimal amount of luck, combined with a modicum of brainpower, will enable a wraith to elude the Harvest• ers until such time as she is able to ward off their depredations. Many wraiths are Reaped by friendlier guides into the afterlife; some even manage to free themselves from their Cauls. Furthermore, Stygian law prevents the forging of any souls except Spectres, criminals and Thralls. On the other hand, the definitions of"criminal" and "Thrall" are conveniently loose for most Reapers to make themselves a tidy profit. Short of Oblivion, there is no more permanent doom for a wraith than soulforging, and most will take their chances in the Void rather than in the forge.

TheConstructionof theUnderworla
Known by many names bur never mapped, the Underworld does nor exist as a "place" in the way that the living view geography. Instead, it is a non-Euclidean nightmare co any who chink that a straight line is still the distance between two pomrs. Even most wraiths don't quire understand che nature of the realm in which they dwell; they tend ro resort co models of increasing simplicity and inaccuracy in order to explain their surroundings.
Also known as the Dark or Low Umbra cosome, the Underworld is best under­ stood as consisting of layers chat, likeC.S. Lewis' mythical kingdom of Narnia, grow larger the further in you go.The outermost one, called the Shadowlands, is identical in location ro the lands of the living. Anywhere the living go the dea<l go as well, and only the metaphysical Shroud separates the two. Theoretically, a wraith could
■	■
walk from Canton, Ohio to Canton, China without ever leavmg the Shadowlands. All that would be required would be patience and a good set of relic boots.
Existing just "below" the surface of the Shadowlands is the eternal turbulence called the Tempest, a roiling, nightmarish storm that has raged unceasingly for half a millennium. No matter whereone goes in the Shadowlands, the Tempest is no more than a slight rip in the fabric of realicy away. Indeed, the Tempest is best thought of as a sort of hyperspace underlying the "reality" of the Shadowlands.
Snaking through this nightmare of a storm are paths of relative safecy called Byways,leading from point to point within the Shadowlands, or from the Shadowlands to the deeper realms. Travel from point to point through the Tempest wtthour resort• ing coa Byway is also possible, and often is faster than taking one of the winding safe paths. On the other hand, travelers in the storm are exposed to its dangers, and the things that dwell within it.
In additton to safe routes through the Tempest, there are islands of srabilicy within the storm as well. The city ofScygia rests on one such island; the innumerable Far Shores rest on others. In these places the Tempest is covered by a deceivingly placid ocean called the Sunless Sea, across which soul-rraffic once moved unceas• ingly. Other islands of safery exist within the storm as well: Feng-tu, Swar, the Sea Thar Knows No Sun, the Bush of Ghosts, and others, the death-realms of other cultures and societies. Some have dealings with Sryg1a, trading souls or waging war across the seas of the dead.
Deep within rhe storm is the fear-shadowed Labyrinth. No one knows what the walls of this horrifying place are made from, though the few explorers who have returned from wandering it report that the very substance of the passages changes as one walks through. Hospital corridors, mirrored mazes, sluggish flowsof molten rock
- all can be found within the Labyrinth. Here, coo, can be found the slumbering Malfeans, the oldest and most potent Spectres.
At the very bottom of the Underworld rests the entrance to the Void. Those who place the Void at the base of the Tempest do so deliberately; entropy serves as grav1cy here, and Oblivion's manifestation exerts a pull on all things in the Tempest and its isles. The Void is ultimate blackness and destruction, a yawning chasm at the center of the Labyrinth. None who haveentered the Void haveever returned, though the fearful belief of some is that reincarnation awaits on the other side of destruc­ tion. Unfortunately, evidence for this hypothesis is sadly lacking at the moment.

TheShadowlands
The stillborn twin of the lands of the living, the Shadowla.ruls correspond ex­ actly to the Skmlands in terms of geography. A wraith standing in Times Square in the Shadowlands is, in a sense, standing in Times Square in the Skinlands, and runs the nsk of being trampled by mortals who don't see him and rush right over his position.
Everything that exists in the Skinlands exists in the Shadowlands, more or less. The more emotion an object or a place inspired in the livmg lands, the more con­ cretely it appears in the Deadlands, and buildings long gone in the Skinlands still rise on the other side of the Shroud. At once beautiful and terrible, the landscape of

However, these perceptions of the Skinlands are often marred by the touch of death, as wraiths see the creeping decay in all things. Still, even the most Oblivion­ tainted among the living appear healthier and more vital than the lands beyond the Shroud, which are gray and somber wich rotting majesty.
TheShroud
The barrier of disbelief and despair that separates the living lands from the Underworld, che Shroud is what divides death from life. Spontaneously raised during the mysterious catastrophe called the S11ndering, the Shroud 1s what limits the ways m which wraiths can touch the Skinlands. The monger the Shroud is in a given place, the more difficult it is for a wraith to reach through to the Skinlands there.
Strongest in places of disbelief and reason, the Shroud is nearly impenetrable in places such as laboratories and lecture halls. Conversely, in places where belief and fearof death arestrong, the Shroud correspondingly weakens, and wraiths have more play in the lands of the living. Cemeteries and homes with troubled adolescents are often places where the Shroud is particularly thin. Also, there are certain nights when, worldwide, the Shroud weakens. These are the nights when the living would
he well-advised to stay indoors, for all over the world, the ghosts are coming out to play.
Haunts
Home sweet home for the dead, Haunts are places where strong emotion has frayed the fabric of the Shroud thin. Here the Shadowlands and Skinlands almost overlap; here are the places d1at the living superstitiously call "haunted."

It 1s the darker emotions that wear through the Shroud most easily, though strong love, hope or courage will do so as well. Even so, Haunts tend to be found in places of despair, desperation or sorrow. Graveyards, slums, batdefields, abandoned
houses, prisons, nightclubs, lonely crossroads and treacherous curves all frequently serve as Haunts.
Most Haunts are vigorously settled by a wraith or Circle of wraiths and de­ fended to the utmost; as wellsprings of emotion and places of shelter, Haunts are ht,tly sought after. Visitors are usually not welcome. Other wraiths prefer to use their Arcanoi to wear on the Shroud, creating Haunts in places of their choosing. Re­ cently,some enterprising ghosts have experimented with raking their Haunts onlme, though whether or nor they've succeeded is a matter of coniecture.

Sfygia
The Eternal Necropolis of Siygia is the collection of all of the dead dreams of empire mankind has ever wrought. As Carthage and Gomorrah fell, their ghostly ruins were gathered and brought to constructStygia's courtsand towers. When Rome and Byzantium were sacked, their wreckage wassalvaged and brought to build Stygia ever higher. Jerusalem and Paris, London and New York, every Imperial Ciry chat ever was i)i echoed in the architecture of the capital of the dead. Built upon the seven hills of the Isle of Sorrows, Stygia the city and Stygia the empire have become syn­ onymous.
Seen from the bottom, Stygia is a claustrophobic nightmare. The weight of ages has pressed down upon the oldest buildings, and Maelsrrom and invasion have taken their toll. Here the streets are cramped and dirty, and the overhanging edifices are crushed beneath the weight of newer constructions.Only the poor and the desperate can be found here, amidst the wreckage of centuries.
The higher one goes in the city, however, the more astonishing the view be­ comes. Newer building and better-preserved treasure of antiquity are the norm. The Library of Alexandria can be found here, and the shining palaces of the Deachlords. This is where the wealthy and powerful among the dead dwell, in stun­ ning contrast to the despair m the labyrinthine warrens below.
Bounded by a sea wall crafted from thousands of souls, the city is home to mil­ lions more. The wallscannot contain all who wish to dwell in Stygia, and so bridges have been cast across the watersof the Sunless Sea co the Iron Hilb, islands that rise from the 1Yaters nearby. Secondary colonies and edifices occupy these smaller isles, but even in the face of such overcrowding, some buildings m Stygia stand empty. The Great Temple of the Fishers, for example, and Charon's Onyx Tower are aban­ doned; even among the dead they have unpleasant reputations.

TheTempest
To sayonly that the Tempest is rhe eternal storm of the Deadlands is to beguilty of gross understatemenr. Eternally raging with a fury unthinkable in the lands of the living, the Tempest is the raw fury that underlies all of the Shadowlands, separating them from Stygia and the Far Shores. Inhabited by shrieking Spectres and ocher, fouler things, it isa roiling sea of chaos within which time, space and distance cease to have meaning.
The landscape of the Tempest is constantly changing; rains of broken glass, noxious gases, eruptions of boiling pitch and worse are common sights to travelers.
Fragments of realities and memories are constantly mflicted upon those who journey through the endless storm, as each wraith who passes through the Tempest leaves something of himself behind. Even those souls who plummet directly to Oblivion upon death leave a memory, a relic or a feeling floating in the storm, co be encoun­ tered by an unwary traveler at some later date.
Wraiths passing through the Tempest are oftengiven revelations, many of them unwamcd. Visions of Fetters or other lives, premonitions, glimpses of the Skinlands and ocher scenes will intrude on a wraith's consciousness as she navigates the dark­ ness.
There are a few islands and strands of stability within the Tempest. The former include Srygia itself, as well as the myriad Far Shores and the other realms of the dead. Narrower paths of relative safety are called Byways, and they screech between realms in the Underworld. Some connect disparate locations in the Shadowlands while others stretch to Srygia or even the Far Shores. Appearing as rivers or roads wending incongruously through the storm, Byways offer relatively safeand fast travel. The greatest Byway is known as the River of Death, whichsupposedly winds its way across all of the Shadowlands and even to the gates of the Labyrinth itself.
At the heart of the Tempest is the Spectre-haunted maze of the Labyrinth, which according to legend was gnawed from nothingness by Malfeans at the dawn of rime. At the very coreof the Labyrinth, far worse thananyMinotaur could be, is the mouth of the Void, Oblivion's embodiment. Few wraiths have gazed upon it and returned co tell others of cheir impressions.

By strict definition, a Nihil is a rip in the Shadowlands that allows access to the Tempest beneath. Most Nihils are one-time-only events, though many open and closeon regular cycles.Someare permanently open; these are often heavily guarded by whichever wraiths dwell nearby.Just as a Nihil allows a wraith access to the Tem• pci;t from the Shadowlands, ir can allow dwellers in the Tempest a way out. Rumors as co the strange powers of Nihils abound; some say that staring into them causes hallucinations or strengthens the Shadow. Metaphysics
Wraiths are caught in a tightrope existence between life and Oblivion, and their perspective reflects this. Just a Shroud's thickness away from the lands of the li\·ing, they are nonetheless eternally separated from those they love. On the other hand, each action, thought and word is a reaction against Oblivion's steady pull, shouting defiance into the Void. Ir is this balance between life and worse than death that the Restless maintain, for as long as they are able.
Touching the Skinlands is something that most wraiths aspire co, no matter what the Dictum Mommm commands. Doing so, though, is not always easy, as the nature of Restless existence makes reaching across the Shroud difficult. Objects in the Skmlan<ls have an odd sort of solidity to them as far as wraiths are concerned; it is nearly impossible for a wraitl, to move a real-world object, but for a minimal in­ vestment of Corpus, she can simply discorporate and walk right through it. For ex­ ample, if a wraith attempts to push on che front of a parked car, her touch will have no effect, and she will find herself unable to affect the car in the slightest. On the other hand, if the car's parking brakesuddenly slips and the car rolls forward, it will proceed to pass (nearly) harmlessly through her as she discorporaces.
Of course, wraiths have longsincediscovered ways to enforce their wills more con­ cretely on the real world. Certain Arcanoi allow Restless to affect or even enter the Skinlands; hence legends of poltergeists, apparitions and ghosts in the machine. All of these phenomena can be traced co wraiths extending their abilities across the Shroud.

Soulforged Goods
The  forges of the Amficers are always hungry for more souls. Artifacts
Artifact is a catch-all term for items in the Underworld that possessspecial pow­ ers. Certain relicsare also Artifacts, but many Artifacts are created across the Shroud. Exceedingly rare and highly sought after, powerful Artifacts are treasures beyond pnce in rhe lands of che dead.

The pas­ sion of multitudes of Quick ts needed to empower a relic thus; also needed is the belief on the part of those mortals that the object is in some way special. Then the relic-co-be must be destroyed, freeing it to cross the Shroud and manifest in the Underworld. Excalibur, the Grail, the Ark of the Covenant and the original Mme. Guillotineare all examples of relic Artifacts. Unlike most relics, relic Artifacts never fade away.
The other variety of Artifact is the soulforged sort. Items crafted in the Artifi­ cers' forges and invested with Arcanoi, these Artifacts are more common but only relatively so. Masks of office and Anacreons' swords are often Amfaccs of this kmd. Because of the great personal cost of making them, soulforgers are very particular as to whom they craft Anifacts for.
Technically, any soulforged item is an Artifact, but no one ever refers to a soulforged brick as an Artifact. Only unique and powerful items are actually labeled "Artifact ."

Cnarons' law
The Hierarchy has its own law system, with Charon's ancient code of the Dicrum Morcuum at its center. This code (literally crarulated, "The Word of the Dead") was createdduring the Middle Ages to regulate che traffic between mortalsand wraiths. Many wraiths inflicted themselves on the living through skinriding, nightmares, materializing and any number of other horrors. Others masqueraded as demons to deluded would-be sorceren;.With little organized resistance or threat of punishment, these wraiths contin• ued their games until the Church responded with exorcisms and wardmgs.

Charon determined to put a stop to this, and imtituted the Code of Charon. It sought to prorect the living from the abuses of the dead by purring a heavy interdic­ tionon all interaction with mortals. It also made very clear chat only Charonand his empowered designates were permitted to gather and dispatch souls.
The Code iscontinually updated, and theStygian Hierarchy continues to maintain it. M06t Shadowland agentsdon't have rimeor energy to read it, let alone enforce it, and simply concentrate on keeping the peace. Many suspect char as long as they keepup the supply of souls to the homeoffice, it won't matter how many infractions they let go.
Jhe ju iciary
Hierarchy law was created as much to punish as to protect. Most Restless follow the dictates quietly, much as they followed Skinlands law in their breathing days. However, there is an element of fear that comes with obedience, a fear which the Hierarchy does not hesitate to capitalize on in the name of law and order. Stygian officials are quick and frequent co crack down on law-breakers, while many of the overworked Legions and other Hierarchy agents m the Shadowlands tend to ignore minor lawbreakers in the interests of keeping the peace and going after bigger fish.
Those whodo break the law come before a Hierarchy magimace costand trial. A wraith may appeal to appear before a "higher" magistrate, but appeals are rarely heard unless the accused has some standing in the local Hierarchy. In some cases a judgment may be appealed co rhe Hand of Fate, and should the Ladies of Fate over­ rum the decision, the Hierarchy will in mosc cases abide by che new ruling. Few know what happens to the defendants in cases that are not dealt w1ch so politely.
Sentencing and punishment
If a wraith breaks a law and is found guilty of a cnme, at least m Stygian terri­ tory, she is punished for 1t. Obviously certain punishment:J,, like the dead, penalty,
51
mean little to those already Jead, and exiling offenders to the Tempest only risks strengthening Oblivion further. As a result, the Hierarchy has become a lircle more creative in its punishments.
Enthrallment - TI1eoffender is chained with Srygian manacles, making her the Thrall of another wraith, usually a Hierarchy official. This is the most common punishment in Stygian territory, though by no means the harshest.
Torture - Wraiths can heal most ordinary wounds, but Stygian torture is designed to scar the offender for all eternity. Devices of Srygian steel inflict un­ dreamed-of pain, and these wounds arc extremely difficult to heal. Some wraiths emerge from their punishment with their Corpora permanently marred by rhe expe­ rience, but the worst wounds are often the unseen ones. To a creature of Passions, scars left on the Psyche are far more devastating than physical wounds.
Imprisonment - Sometimes criminals are jai.led in Shadowland reflections of mortal prisons, with reinforced wallsand bars of Srygian metal to prevent escape. This devastating punishment works in several ways. First of all, it keeps the wraith from visiting her Fetters or renewing her Passions, weakening her ability to resist her darker half. In addinon, when a wraith is left alone in the dark to brood, her Shadow will inevitably redouble its assaults on her Psyche.

Branding- Branding isa way to mark criminals, effectivelyostracizing them from society and warning other would-be miscreants from attempting the same. The size and shape of the brands varies according to the severity of the crime. Suious crimes (assuming the offender escapes the forges or the Masquers) result in the of­ fender wearing her brand on her forehead, exposing her to relentless scorn.
Discorporation - This is the most severe punishment wtthin the Hierarchy's power. The offender is transported to the nearest forges and smelted down for soulgoods. Alternately, some criminals are turned over to wraithsskilled in Moliate and reshaped into other "useful" implements.

T e Brea ing
Despite the problems in its inception, the Council unified the Guilds as a po­ litical force. While they bickered behind closed doors, they presented a united front to the rest of Srygia. They towered over rhe rest of the Stygian population in power, surpassed only by the Deathlords (no doubt to those august wraiths' collective re­ lief). However, the winds of change were blowing, particularly in the form of unrest among the Hereucs and Renegades. Certain Guildmasters saw the instability wrought by these groups as a chance to seize power from Charon's obviously weakened grasp.
On April 6, 1598, the Guilds, led by the Artificers, attempted to stage a coup d'ecat.
The Usurers had helped to begin the revolt, but abandoned the project suspiciously quickly. When they left, their closest allies, the Masquers, followed, and the coup began to founder. It ended a miserable failure, and many of che offenders were oblit­ erated by the next tide.
In the wake of the rebellion, Charon had concerns that too much power was being concentrated in the Guilds' grasp. Another, more organized coup might well have succeeded where the Artificers' clumsy power grab had failed. He decided to remove the threatof usurpation bydissolving that threatening concentration of power, and did so by banishing the Freewraichs' Guilds from Stygia.
The Decree of the Breaking, as it was called, outlined Charon's policy agalllst the GuHds, to wit: The existing Guilds were dissolved as official organizations; any groups calling themselves Guilds were outlawed in Stygian lands; Guild membership was a crime; and specially trained Legionnaires would be taking the place of Guild members in Stygian society. Like the coup, the Decree was misguided in intention and doomed co failure. After 20 years of sabotage and work stoppages, Charon dis­ covered the Le ions could not replace the Guilds. For chat matter, the Breaking had done nothing to slow down Guild members themselves. They stilt had the tools and the talents for which wraiths would pay handsomely.

Still,1c would have made for a bad precedent for Charon to reverse his decision. Such an about-face would have suggested chat he could neither create flITTl policy mandates nor enforce his will, and m the post-coup instability, appearing weak in front of the predatory Deathlords would have incited more rebellions. In the end, he chose to reinstate "former" Guild members individually on the condition that they underscood the Guilds were no more. However, the fiction that the Guilds were destroyed wasstill circulated among the populace, and eventually the populace grew to believe 1t.

cforc youcan begin to play Oblivion, you must create a character. This character will be the role you assume in the stories you and your friends tell. Unlike make-believe, you don't just make up a character osyou goalong. Instead, you create the character before you start playing. There's a certain amount of work involved - characters are made, not born. Character creation is a creative sll'\.lggle,and even the most experienced roleplayers find it a chal­ lenge to build a compelling yet honest character.
This chapter describes how to create a unique character,starting with a general concept and translating that concept into values that can be used in the game. The process itself is very simple, but you should not hesitate to ask a Narrator any ques­ tions you might have.
Character creation is like cooking. You gather the ingredients, sur them together, and let the result bakefor a while. Youstart by deciding what kind ofcharacter you want.
Arcyou goingto bea street-hardened punk,or a richandspoiled ex-deburanre? Are you a college graduate, or were you tutored in life somewhere in the Appalachian Moun­ tains?The background and personality of your character are the essential ingredients of
h1Sor her persona. From your basic concepc;, you can work out the derails.
Getting Started
You need to decide what type of character you want to play. Were you an honl'St flolice officer who made a mistake in dealing with a criminal, and who wasshot and killed

•.	olivion
...j·T,- c·s.="'
i!I.)
'I  A��
in the line of duty? Maybe you were a solid citi2en who died senselessly m an auto acci­ dent? Were youa famous celebrity whodied ofa twin OD of heroin and fame? Maybe you werea fan ofa celebrity who killedhimself, causing you to hang yourself in grief? The way you livedanddied helps to make up yourcharacter,as does your wrairh's major regret from life. That an cum makes up yourcharacter's background and personality, and, when added ro your character's statistics, rhey can createa well-developed and well-rounded wraith.
You can create a character at any age. Her culture and background is entirely up to you (with the approval of the Storyteller, of course). However, you scan the game as a fairly young and inexperienced Lemure, a newly created wraith. You have only recently crossed the Shroud and joined the ranks of the Restless Dead. You know little of ghostly society or unlife, except what you've been told by those few ghoscs you've already met in your travels, and perhaps by your Reaper. ln any case,you have been a wraith for 50 years or less. Your apparent age is the age when you "died."

The Storyteller may restrict your character creation choices or offer you other choices besides those listed here. Many stories have special considerations chat must be taken into account. There will often be certain roles that need to be filled in a story, and you may have to create a character co fill that need.
Oblivion's character creation process is designed as much to help you define your character as it is ro provide you with a means to interact with the rules. The processes of creacmg the character and picking yourstrong and weak poincs are meant co help you tighten che concept of your character.
Thisgame'scharacter creation system isa selectionsystem. BychoosingTraits(quali­ tiesdescribing yourcharacter) from a seriesofliscs, you build the persona you're going co play. le IS best co list all the Traitsand qualities you would like yourcharacter to haveand then eliminate the ones chat aren't essential to your concept. Unfortunately, you can't always have everything you wane. After all, neither life nor death is fair.
It is your responsibility to createa character who suirs the story. In this, you are guided by the Storyteller. A hard-edged street chronicle probably has no place for the wraith of a nice littleold lady whose Passions involve helpingchildren across che street. lf your character doesn't have a place in the story, you have co create a new character or carve out your own place in the existing tale. The latter can be much harder, and runs the risk of making the game lessenjoyable for other players if in che end your character just doesn't fir.
Cnaracter Creation
he  following is a breakdown of the character creation process.
Step One: Inspiration
Before you write anything down on your character sheet, you need to  find inspiration for the character you want to play. Once you've gotten che inspiration that you have been look­ ing for, you can start todevelop the basic groundwork.
Character development involves choosinga concept (how you lived and died, and what is yt,ur greatest regret from your living days) and a personality (by chQ(lSing a Na•

StepTwo:Attriutes
Acrributes are everything a character naturally, intrinsically is. Are you strong1 Are you br:we?Are you persuasive!Questionssuch as theseare aruwered by the way you distrib­ utt your Attributes, the Traits that describe the basic, innate potential of your character.
The nn;rstep is to prioritize the threedifferentcategoriesof Attributes, placing them in orderof importance to yourcharacter. Are you moreadept at physical matters than you are m social sitllations? Docs your intellectsurpass your physical prowess, or vice versa?
Physical Attributes describe the abilities of the body, such asstrength, dexter­
ity and endurance.
Social Attributes describe your character's appearance and charisma - her ability to influence others.
Mcneal Attributes represent yourcharacter's mental capacity and includesuch thing as memory, perception, self-control and the ability to learn and think.
The concept and affiliations of your character may suggest what your Amibute pnoritiei;should be, but feel free to pick any way you please. For now, think in the broad­ est of perspectives- youcan get morespecific after you undersrand the big picwre.
After you've chosen che order of your wraith's three Attribute categories, you need to choose specific Traits from each category. Traits are adjectives that describe your character'sstrengths and weaknesses, defining yourcharacter JUStasa character ina novel 1Sdd1ned. ln your primary (strongest) Attribute category, youcanchoose sevenTraits. In your e,econdarycategory, youcan choose five. In your tertiary (weakest) category, youcan llllc.e only three. Thus, you receive a total of 15 Attribute Traits. You can take the same Trait more than once, if you wish, reflectinggreater aptitude at a particular specialty.

Step Three: Advantages
Advantages are the Traits that separate one character from another. They allow a character co cake acrions that would be impossible to a normal human being. There are four categories of advantages: Abil,ries, Arcanoi, Passion,, and Fetters.
choosing Abilities
Abilities represent your craming and knowledge beyond the outline provided by your Attributes. They are what you have learned and what youcan Jo rather than what you are. Abilities let you perform specialized rasks that are only possible with training: hacking computers, soulforging or reading the Dead Sea Scrolls.

Choose five different Abilities from the list that follows.

c oosingArcanoi
Arcanoi are gho:,cly powers, the supernatural abiltries available co a wraith. Each wra1rh begins thegame with three Arcanoi, and you maychoose any Arcanoi to start the game. Each Arcanos has two powers ac the basic level. If the character hasan Arcanos, the first power listed must be chosen first.The character cannot advance co intem1e<.li­ nte-level powers in a given Arcanos until all of the beginning powers of that Arcanos h,we been learned. Coosing passions
Passions are whar fuel a wraith's Underworld existence. By harvesting Pathos trom )Our Pasi,ioru;, you can heal yourself or even use your Arcanoi. You may choose four Passions, focused emotions which drive your existence and give you strength.
When selecting Pai.sions, you must phrase the Passion itself as an imperative and then dL-ci<le what emotion liesat itscoreof ic.If yourcharacter wasmurden:d, you might
67
have the Passion Avenge my murder (Hate). Remember that the obvious emotion fora Passion is not always the appropriate one; if your character's murder might lead to the deaths of others you love, you might have the Passion Avenge my murder (Fear).Then
again, you might simply wane to get back at the person who bumped you off, in whtch case the Passion Avenge my murder (Vengeance) might be more appropriate.

This may be overseen by a Narrator, or may simply be a test between the wraith and a bystander if no Narrator can be found.
C oosing fetters
Fetters are people, places or other material objects that simultaneously keepa wraith attached co the Skinlands and anchor him against Oblivion's relentless pull. In fact, Fetters are partof what makes a lost soul a wraith. Each represents something that held a special meaning or certain importance to your character while he was alive. In practical terms, your Arcanoi are often easier co use around your Fetters; plus, you can rest and heal more easily near a Fetter.
You get to choose four Fetters, describing each one exactly. Bear in mind that things happen duringgameplay which cannot be foreseen; you might lose a Fetter or gain one, depending upon how things work out.
Stepfour:shadow Creation
Thereare twomain aspects to a wraith's personality: the Psyche and the Shadow. The Psyche is the main pare of a wraith's personality, the one that represents his force of will and identity. The Shadow is the dark side of his personality, the voice in the back of his head that caioles, connives and corrupts him to do its bidding until it drags him down into Oblivion.

As it 1s impossible to have players take the part of each others' Shadows in Oblivion (what to do if your Shadow is on the other side of the building? Take ad­ vantage, of course....), therole falls to Narrators. With that in mind, you should write down the details of your Shadow on a Shadow sheet (provided at the back of the book) and carry that sheet wid, you at all times when you play Oblivion. When a Shadowguiding Narrator arrives on the scene and decides to rake the part of your Shadow, you'll hand over yourShadow sheet to that Narrator.Shadows can connive, strike deals and otherwise interfere with your character's everyday existence.
Complete descriptions of Shadow creation and systems for Shadowguiding are in Chapter Six.
Arc etypes
Just as the Psyche has Archetypes, the Shadow does as well. Shadow Archetypes, however, are warped and d1St0rted. These Archetypes, just like your Psyche's Nature and Demeanor, define your wraith'sShadow and help make that part easier co roleplay. Your Shadow's Archetype also uggests the sore of tactics and entreaties it will make of your Psyche, not to mention whac it might do if it ever gets to takeover.
Angst is the Shadow equivalent coa wraith Pathos. Angst refleclll the so-ength of thecorrupting voice inside your head in tenns of how loud it is and how much control it hasoveryou. Angst comes in twotypes:Temporary and Pennanent. The Shadowacquires Angst m:;everal ways, includingfeeding its Dark Passions and accumulating 10 poinlll of Temporary Angst ro gam one Permanent AngstTrait. Angst is the fuel chat yourShadow uses to work its way with you as it tries to steer you ever closer to Oblivion embrace.
More on Angst, its uses and costs can be found in Chapter Six.
Dart passions
Thl' Shadow's goals and drives are embodied in Dark Passions, which are usu­ ally the oppo:,ite or corrupted, self-destructive versions of the Psyche's normal Pas­ sions. For example, if you had a Passion such as Avenge my murder (Anger), your Shadow's Dark Passion might be Get revenge on anyone who disagrees with me (Hate). There is a fine lme between anger and hate, but if your character ever seeks excessive vengeance on another wraith, that might be enough for a test to see if your Shadow gains Temporary Angst. More information concerning Dark Passions and tht.>tr use in roleplaying can be found in Chapters Four and Six.

Healt
Characters are considered to be at full health at the beginning of each story unless the Storyceller states otherwise. Of course, characters can be hurt or even destroyed duringastory. All wraithsstart eachgame with a Permanent Corpus rating of 10, bur this can be reduced through Harrowings.
StepSix:Spark of Deatn
There are other aspects of a character that should be detailed. These flourishes are not necessarily important in terms of the game, but are vital with regard co roleplaying. In many cases, these "sparks" are provided for you, or at least suggested to you, by the Storyteller. Your character needs to be woven into the story, and these "sparks" allow the Storyteller codo just that.
Ot er Aspects
Background- You need cocreate a background foryourcharacter, describing his life before dyingand becominga wraith: what he did, how he lived and what was unique about him.This background may describe what yourcharacter did fora living, howhesaw himselfand what others thought of him. Indeed, many wraiths find it difficult to abandon their conceptsof themselves asliving beings, andclingto trappings of their former lives in their perceptions of themselves and their dress. Their past remains with them forever.
Regardless of when youdied,whether it wascenturies agoduringthe AgeofReason or lasrThursday, you havespent 30 yearsor lessasa "free"wraith.lf you areover30 yearsdead as a wraith, you need a reason, such as being finally released from shdtered servitude, Thralldom to one of the three major factions (which might give you a good reason for having ties to that particular faction),or someother reason ofwhich a Storyteller approves.
Secrets - Each character has secrets of some sort, things that she doesn't want ochers to discover. One secret that almost all wraiths posses:; is the location of their per­ sonal haunts; who needs Reapers pokingaround while you'reSlumbering?TheStoryteller is likely to give you a number of secrets that you need to protect over the cowse of the chronicle.

•Motivations-What isyour purpose?What motivatesyouon a<lay-to-day, night• to-night basis? Describe your motivations in as muchdetail as possible; ask the Storyceller forhelp ifyou can't thinkofanything. Unlessyou arean experienced player, it'slikely that the Storyteller will provide you with a motivation or two at the start of the chronicle.
Appearance - Props and costumes will help ochers understand, or at least recognize, your character at a glance. You need not only to act like your character,
t,uc look like him as well. Your character's appearance makes his Physical (and many Social) Tra1cs visible to other players.
A wraith's appearance IS constructed from his perception of himself. Thts means rhat many Resrless look similar, but not idenrical, co the ways they did when they were among the living. Wraiths often appear in clothing more appropriate to the year in which theydied than the current one.Some wraiths'appearances havechanged Jramancally from cheir living visages, whether through the effectsofMoliate or merely rhe1r own self-perceptions. lf your character is a wraith of this sort, you will need to finJ a way to let others know that your character's appearance isJifferent than yours.
Many wraiths wear masks, either as badges of office or as fashion statements. You may wish to create a personalized mask for your character that indicates things like her affiliation, rank, etc.
Equipment - Your character is likely co begin rhe game with equipment of one sort or another. Ask a Narracor for more details on your personal possessions, known in the Underworld as relics. Relics are the memories of items that have been either destroyed or damaged to the point that they can never be fixed. Through synchronic1ry or wraithly manipulation, some of these have crossed the Shroud to l:>ecome relics. The assortment of relics available is somewhat haphazard. There are many relic guns, but relic bullets are scarce. Relic cars abound, bur few work.
Youcan choose relicsappropriate to yourcharacter's background, checking with che Storyteller co see if what you choose is appropriate to the chronicle. Relic teddy bears, lockets and the like should be all right regardless of circumstance; relic anti, cank weapons are usually not.
Items created in the Underworld and relics imbued with special abilities are called Artifacts. Arcifaccs are nor considered normal equipment for characters no matter what, as they must be purchai;ed during character creation.
Quirks- By giving your character quirks (interesting personality characteristics), you add a great deal of depthand interest to her. Writea fewsentenceson the back of your charactersheetabout the strange and interesring things thatdefine yourcharacter. Examples of 4uirb include a morbid senseof humor, an obsession for collecting lost relic inglesocks, or a habit of grunting when answering yes to a question. These little couches area great way to personalize yourwraith, and to make her come ualive to you (so tospeak).
Advanceddaracter Creation
Sometimes the Storyteller may want players to create characters whoare not your average just-starting-outwraith. Insuchcases,additional Traits may be provided in many categories, and limics on Negative Traits may be raised. Rules for
such"advanced" character creation are provided in Chapter Four. Ask your Storyteller for more details.

Traits
hen a writerdoe,;n't have the timeor need to demonstrate what a character is like, descnpcive words are used. These words mu:,t he carefully selected so that they reveal all the important features of
' the character', persona.These words are the adjectives: "Violerca was a gracef11l,auracti1,e woman - beguiling co look upon, but cal· lous and !llCdess when engaged in conversation." In Oblivion we use these adjectives in much the same way. Indeed, they are the basisof the game system. We call them Traits.
Traits have two primary purposes. The first purpose 1s to enable you to describe your character concretely and thereby empower your roleplaying. The second is to enable you to mreracc with other characters by means of the mechanics of the game system. The mechanics of Oblivion revolve around the Trait system. Challenges becween characters are resolved using chem.

The premise of thissyotem is that a character who isdescribed bya specificTrait rends
to be pretty gooJ at things that involve that Trait, and is certainly better at those things
C a ter four:Cl1arncteristics
han,omeone whodoom't have thatTrait. Forexample,someone who IS Brawny is a better 1rm-wrestler than someone who isn't. Likewise, a marathon runner needs to be Trreless in r l\l fini:,h a racewithout beingwinded, and a child who IS Persuasive hasa good chance ofamvincing his mom that hedidn't break the vase that's been shattered on the floor.
In thoory, a player can choose any adjective and take it as a Trait. However, for a numl-er of reasons, this is notsuggested for any but the most expert players. Man}'adjec­ nve,,are unimportant, extraneous and/or impractical; there is no nee<l for an Obsrreper­ ow Trott, a:, all itdoes isduplicate the effect ofseveral othersalready in place.
Allowmg anyadjective would play havoc with the mechanics of the game. Players would have to carry around pocket dictionaries and flip pages every time some En­ gli,h maior bid the TraitVigesimal. If there are a set number of standard adjectives u,ed as rhe Trait pool, everyone can be expected to know the meaning of every adje-w,·e and can know when each is applicable. For these reasons, Oblivion has a ho1 of tandard Traits from which to choose.
If you decide to add more Traits to the list, here are some guidelines: keep the number of new Traits low, make sure all players understand them, and make sure the} Jon'r duplicate existing Traits.
Attri utes and BiddingTraits
Creative players can think of ways to use nearly any Trait m nearly any challenge. Though this is praiseworthy, people can sometime go tOO far. Toavoid this, the general ruleon hidding Traits is very strict: You can only bid Traits from the category that best su1rsLhe narure tif the challenge (i.e., bidding isdone using oneof the three basic carego­ ril'S- Physical, Mental or Social). Even then, however, not all Physical (or Mental, or Social) Traitsare appropriate to all Physical (or Mental, or Social) Challenges.
Beginning players might thmk they can use all their Physical Traits in combat. This is incorrecr. If your character is trying to kicksomeone, Resilient is nor an appro­ priate Trair co bid as part of ihe attack. Likewise, if your character is trying to figure our if someone i.:; a Spectre, Creauve might not be an appropriate Trait.
Forsuch an "inappropriate" Trait to be allowed, both parties must agree. When an opponent bid a Tran that you feel is extremely inappropriate, politely tell him that you're not gomg to allow its use. lfhe is insistent, reevaluate your grievance. lf you still can't agree, appeal roany wimess of the comest. Then, if there is still deadlock and no one iswilling to compromise, seek oura Narrator to make a ruling. Appeals roa Narrator, however, should occur very, very rarely. Learn to handle confrontation:. on your own, quicklyand without interplayer conflict. Oblivion isa social game, andquick resolutions ofout-of-game maners makes the game more entertaining for everyone.

Abilities
Abilines are the skills, talentsand knowledges of the characters in a live-action roleplaymg environment. Obviously, some do not translate well or do not apply co Oblivion. Others have been incorporated into the Attributes of a character and can he performed by executing challenges.
Abilities allow the character to engage in, if not excel m, a type of challenge m whichshe could not otherwise participate. Addttionally, if a character is defeated in sucha challenge, she may choose to sacrifice a level in the appropriate Ability to call for a retest. While any Traits risked are still lose, it is possible to still win the chal­ lenge. An Ability lost in this manner is recovered at the beginning of the next ses­ sion. If a character loses all her levels man Ability m this manner, she may not use that Ability until the levels are recovered.

Drive
h 1s assumed that all modem characters have a base familiarity with Drive, allowing them to handle an automobile with reasonable ease in normal situations. Instead, Drive allows you to perform tricky maneuvers, drive at high speeds and en­ gage in car chases. Arcanoi
Art thou 1101, faraLvision, sensible To feelingas 10 sight? or ari ihou but A dagger of dte mind....
- William Shakespeare, Macbedt
Death opens a door to things unknowable by the living. There are arcane paw­ as onlv available to rhe Restless, known as Arcanoi (singular, Arcanos). Arcanoi are secrets that can be either learned at the foot of another or acquired through expencnce. Theyare ways of using a wraith's spiritual being to airer other aspects of the Underworld, or m some cases che Skinlands. In many ways, an Arcanos is as much a philosophy as a craft, made of equal parts viewpoint and skill. Mose wraiths demonstrate apcirude for one or rwo Arcano1 immediately upon their rebirth, and quick! learn the first few pawers, or arts, of those Arcanoi.
Submerging oneself m the practice of an Arcanos can mark a wraith with traces of that power. These marks make her recognizable as one who is well-versed in a specific arcane skill, and cannot be removed by cosmetic alteration. The details vary from Arcanos to Arcanos.
Excessive useof an Arcanos will mark a wraith permanently, whether or not she is a member of the Guild associated with that particular Arcanos. A wraith diving into theTempe•t with Argos on a regular basis will acquire jet-black eyes, even ifshe 1w ne\•er met an "official" Harbinger. It is the Arcanos itself (or the environment associated with its use) that creates the markings, not any sort of Guild association.

However, there are many wraiths who have learned more than one Arcanos co such an extenL that they start developmg more than one sel of distinctive marks. This is no longer uncommon in a Scygia where average citizens are encouraged to learn multiple Arcano,. Usually, the multi-talented wraith will manifest the dimnc­ Uve patterns of all the Arcanoi she uses, unless there issome conflict between the
developing marks (1.e., Argos and lnt1mation modify the appearance of the user's eyes m murually exclusive ways). In this case, the markings of the Arcanos that is USed more frequently will predommate, though some evidence of the lesscommonly arts will trianifest as well. For example, a Harbinger with some knowledge of timauon (which stains its user's left eye bnght green) will have the traditional set
0 Jct-black eye,, but with a distinctly green sheen to his left one.

passions
assions are at the core of every wraith's existence. In a very real sense, a wraith's Passions are what keeps her going. She musr strive co fulfill them, otherwise they will wither. Howe1•er, if she attends ro chem, she can reap a harvest of Pathos, enahling her co continue her ghostly journey.
As stared m Chapter Three, a player muse choose four Passions char fie her character's personality, background and history. To regain Pathos by means of a Passion, the character
must either evoke the emotion at the center of the Passion in another or feel char emotion strongly hen;elf. Sometimes a wraith will change the environment m evoke the desired emotion in spectacors; other times she will simply strive to follow the Passion itself Eicher path, w1th Narrator approval, can lead co rhe wraith gaming additional Pathos Traits, up ro a maximum of 10.
ln any scene where a character evokes an emotion in someone else, rhe player makes an Extended Simple Test to sec if she can sympathize with the emotions of those around her. Onl.y with a win m the Simple Test can she receive a wraith's spincual energy called Pathos, where each win allows her cogain another point of Pathos. If a Narrator is present, :.he must approve the Pathos gain hy verifying che sincemy of the emotion felt or invoked. If no Narrator is present, the consent of all players present is necessary for a test agamst a Passion. No Passion can be re.seed against more than once every two hours; codo so more frequently would be cocheapen the Passion in quemon. If a wraith fails a rest to gain Pathos from a Passion, char Passion ts considered to be Numlx.-d for the duration of the evenmg. Numbed Pas· sions cannot be tested against; however they are recovered ar the beginning of the next evening.

Drawing Pathos from unfocused emotion can be difficult. Feelings are some• times impure, tinged by conflicting drives, and a wraith's sympathy with these moo· grel emorions is not always complete. However, when a wraith isdirectly involved in_ following the dictates of her Passion, she can draw upon extraordinary reserves ot strength. In cases where the wraith is lircrally pursuing one of her Pas ions (in che Judgment of rhe Narrator), an Extended Stmple Test allows the wratth co conrinue co retest and gain Pathos 1f she ties at any pomc.
Some sample emotions that can serve as the base of Pass1om include love, an­
)!',faith, hope, envy, lust, pnde, sorrow, pam and greed.
Some examples of Passions are:
Anger: Fight injustice Avenge your death Punish all spousal abusers
Overthrow a corrupt politician
Fight the criminal syndicate that destroyed your neighborhood
Love: Find a new owner for a cherilihed item Protect your daughter
Declare a love that you couldn't before
Help your true love find someone else
Greed: Possess a particular valuable item Serve the Hierarchy for payment
Protect the family fortune
Lust: Experience gratification that you never knew m life Live vicariously through those you knew before dying
Faith: Become a martyr for a cause you know is right Foster belief in a higher power
Help your family to keep their faith
Hope: Protect the oppressed Protect a homeless helter
Prevent a loved one from committing suicide
Sorrow: Make your Haunt a place of remorse Give gnef to your enemy
Th,.. wraith will nor always be able to work specifically coward her purposes as tared in her Passions. After all, although she can fight ro maintain her ties to her former life, she has other concerns: the stability of her Circle, politics in her Necropolis, herShadow's encroachments and so on. If a wraith neglectsa Passion for IDO long, however, ac Storyteller discretion it can become Numbed, and require a Willpower Trait and a Pathos pomt to be reactivated. Passions that remain Numbed fur three consecutive game sessions are considered co have faded. The Passion is
snicken from the character sheet, and the character herself falls intoa Harrowing. In addition, purposes are sometimes completed. lf a wraith seeks to protect her
.mortal lover and he dies, she will no longer be able to fulfill that Passion. At this
poinc, she would also fall intoa Harrowing.
FadL-d Passions can only be replaced at Storyteller discretion. A wraith who lose­
her last P ton, however, falls into the Void, and the character is removed from play.
ratltos
Pathos is pure emotional energy, harvested by wraiths from their Passions an<l
lllled to fuel their existences. Pathos powers Arcanoi, heals Corpus damage, and serves
,Is SUstenance to wraiths.
'
olivion
A wraith's Pathos Pool often fluctuates, depending on how often he makes USe of his Arcanoi or needs co heal. It is entirely possible for a wraith to find himself completely out of Pathos; chisdoes not mduce a Harrowing, but will leave him more or less defenseless until he is able to exercise one or more of his Passions.

AU wraiths regain one point of Path_os at the beginnmg of each night ofgameplay.
fetters
Fetters are material objects bindmg a wraith to the Skinlands. Theyare, mfact part of what makes a lost soul a wraith. Each Fetter represents something, someon: or someplace that was important to the wraith in life. The shot glass with your name on it, kepr behind the counter at your favorite bar, may be a Fetter, but it represents more than might be immediately apparent; perhaps you spent much of your time drinking from that very glass, ignoring your family. Take great care in choosing your Fetters, for they can provide a wealth of information about your character as well as a myriad of ideas for potential plots.
A wraith starts off with four Fetters. During character creation he should de­ scribeexactly what each is and what it means to him.This list should then be turned over to the Storyteller. The listofcharacter Fetters in a gameshould always beacces­ sible to aU Narrators and Storytellers; you never know when a Momtor might beon the loose.
Fetter are what anchor a wraith to the Skinlands. If any of his Fetters is de­ stroyed, the wraith is Harrowed. Objects that are Fetters should be marked as such, characters who are Fetters should be notified of such, and should either be killed/ destroyed, a Narrator must be notified immediately. It 1s then chat Narracor's respon­ sibility to find the character connected to the Fetter and lead him to a Harrowing.
