Challenges
Dunng the course of most stories, there will come a time when two or more players will come intoa conflict that cannot be resolved simplyand quickly. It doesn't matter whether thissort ofconflict isa firefight or a test of will. Thisface-off iscalled a challenge. The system for resolving challenges in Oblivion is simple, and 10 most cases, a Narrator does not need co be present when a challenge is played.

Roleplaying does not necessarily have to end when a challenge begins. Experi­ enced players can seamlessly integrate a challenge into their roleplaying so tharout­ siders don't know that anything unusual is going on. At the player's option, hand signals can be used to indicate when certain Traits and powers are being employL.J.
Finally, in order for this system to work, players need to work together. Players need ro educate each other on the rules and agree on what Traits can he used in a challenge. Compromise and cooperation are the bywords of the game.
The challenge system presented in this chapter is also part of the basic rule,for rhe Mind's Eye Theatre system. By combmmg The Oblivion with olher games in the series, players can have wraiths interact w1rh vampires, werewolves, mortals and other types of characters. This system of challenges is also included in Masquerade Second Edition and Apoca.lypse.

Experience
I've seen coo much I've heard enough...
A lit[le innocence always seems wgo 'long the way
-  Cock Robin, "A Little lnnocence"
s time passes, people (living or dead) grow and learn. They acquire new skills, talentsand knowledge. To reflect that, char­ acters in Oblivion receive Experience Traits as they soiourn through rhe afterlife. The more theysee and do, the more they know and know how to do, and Experience Traits reflect this.
While Experience Traits themselves do nothing, theycan be traded in for other, more useful characteristics. A sufficient number of Experience Tra1rs can be swapped for new Pas.ion,,
Fetters, Pathos, Willpower, Traits, Abilities and even Arcanoi. When you wanr to swap Experience Traitsforsomerhmgelse,simply inform your Storyteller what you're buying and how much Expenence you're cashing in.
Cnapterri\'e: ules
Different characteristicscost different amounts of Expenence.
New Attribute Trait -  One experience point per Trait.
New Abiliry - One experience point per Ability Trait.
New Arcanos - Two experience point5 for Innate Abilities, four for Basic Arcanoi, six for Intermediate Arcanoi and nine for Advanced Arcanoi. Bemg ap­ prenticed within a Guild (see Guild Status, p. l37) reduces the cost by one for all Arcanos levels except Innate Abilities.
New Willpower -  Three experience points per Trait.
Buy off Negative Trait - Two experience points per Trait.
New Background - One expenence point per Influence Trait, with Story­ teller approval.
Pathos - Two Traits per experience point.
GettingExperience
Characters gain one Experience Trait at the end of each game. In addition, Storytellers have the option of handing out additional Experience to players as well for exceptional roleplaying. Players should always gain at least one Experience Trait unless they really, truly, vehemently prove that they do not deserve it (say, byspend­ ing the entire game playing a Spectre who says nothing but, "&rk! l½,rk! &rkl"). Otherwise, participation is enough ro grant some Experience.

hadowguiding is an essenrial part of any game of Oblivion, for the Shadowgu1de serves to express the dark urges of a wraith's lower self- thevoice inside his head trying to lead che wraith into the Void. The Shadow is violent and selfish, cunning and cowardly - the ultimate combination of the office snitch and the schoolyard bully. It can be quieted but never sulleJ, and occasionally it can even take over. In that instance, called Ca­ tharsis, the wraith's true personality issuddenly shunted to the
back of hi:.own mind, 1mpnsoneJ as the Shadow controls their shared body.

In Oblivion,Shadowguiding is che responsib1liryof Narrators. Each player haa sheet detailing her Shadow's Archerype, Thorns, Dark Passions and Angst (Perma­ nent and Temporary). Narrators on Shadow dury simply wander the game, findmg wraiths whom they think are due for some attention from their dark side. Whena suitable wraith 1s found, the Narrator taps her on the shoulder and asks her to hand over her Shadow sheet temporarily. Narrato canconcentrate on one wraith, or can take the part of the Shadows of an entire group.
While Shadowguiding, a Narrator should generally whisper in the ear of the wraith she 1s tormenting. No one can hear the Shadow's words except the wraith, so none of the other players should hear them as well. A Shadowguide should never touch the wraith she'sdealing with, norshould she addrei;s any ocher players directly, bur she can interrupt gameplay to announce the effect of a Thorn.
shadowguiding Do'sand Don'ts
Avoid annoying the player whose Shadow you control. Shadow the charac­ ter, not the player.
Choose your opportunities for temptation carefully. Rather than broadsidmg players with general enticements, find out what makes chem tick and exploit chose weaknesses at appropriate times.
Don't focus your Shadowguiding too much on one character. There are a lot of wraiths itching for temptation out there; if you spend coo much time on one, his player is going to feel persecuted.
Embarrassing another player is not fun for anyone. Try to avoid abusing your power as Shadowguide ro cause discomfort to the player.
Find out if the player whose Shadow you control has a problem dealing with certain topics or phobias, and avoid those when you are acting as Shadowguide. Do nor spoil the game for another player.
Keep in mind that the Shadow you are playing isa small pan of a largergame, and
try co keep your actions directed to increasing the drama and enjoyment of the game.
Remember that the player of a wraith's Psyche controls her character regard· less of whether or not the Psyche or the Shadow is dominant. Do not attempt to play someone else's character iusr because you are playing her Shadow.
Try co make your Shadow's words and actions enterraining as well as effecuve. Others are watching what you do.

psychic Torment
The Shadow has only one long-term goal - to be united with Oblivion - but there's the little macrer of the wraith's dominant personalit, and how to deal with that. While the Shadow mayseem helpful on occasion, its ultimate goal never wavers. However, there are a variety of stratagems that Shadows will employ (from offering helpand then withdrawing it at crucial momenrs, to promising quiet in exchange for cemun actions, to all-out 24/7 war), and not every Shadow wants to get the battle over with quickly. After all, ic can be so much more fun to take years to achieve corruption artistically, cosend all of the wraiths' friendsscreaming to the Void first.... With chat in mind, Shadow behavior can realistically be seen to run the gamut. However, with the Shadow, there's always a knife behind the smile.

Angst
Stand back, get back
She'U painc your blue skies black
-Simple Mimis, "She's A River"
Angst represents the degree of pain and isolation a wraith feels, and it also represents the strength of a wraith's Shadow. Angst comes in two varieties: Tempo• rary and Permanent. Permanent Angst is the hold that the Shadow has gamed on the wraith'ssoul, while Temporary is merely a measure of how much negative energy and emotion tbe wraith has at the moment. Permanent Angst changes slowly and always increases; Temporary Angst goes up and down dunng the course of gameplay.
If a Shadow gains 10 Temporary Angst Traits in an evening, it has the option of tradmg them m for either one permanent Angst Trait or a scene of Catharsis (see below). If a wraith's Permanent Angst Traits ever reach 10, she has given in perma­ nently to her Shadow and succumbed co Oblivion. Such wraiths may become Spec­ tres, but they must vanish into the Tempest for the period of transformation.

GainingAngst
There are several ways the Shadow can gain Angsc:
Dark Passions - lf a Shadow is able to manipulate the Psyche or ocher wraiths intoexpressing one of ics Dark Passions, it can call for a Simple Tesc. If cheShadow wms, a pointofTempomry Angst isacquired. A tie indicates a retest until one sideor the ocher actually wins. This method is only pertinent if the Shadow is actually being guided. Otherwise, AngsL can only be gained from Dark Passions during Catharsis. This isdone in the same manner in which a wraith gains Pathos from her regular Passions.
Arcanoi -  Some Arcanoi give the Shadow Temporary Angst pomts even j(
the Psyche is dominant.
Note: If the Shadow is dominant and uses an Arcanos rhar normally gives the Psyche TempoTary Angst, that Angst is lost instead.
Roleplaying- If a player docs an excellent jobat Cathams, a Storyteller can (with player permission) give the Shadow a point of Temporary Angst. Such supe­ rior roleplaying should also be rewarded wich extra Experience.
losing Angst
There are several ways the Shadow can lose Angst:
Castigation - This Arcanos isdesigned co weaken the power a Shadow holds over a wraith by reducing the wraith's Angst. Unless a wraith 1s using the advanced level of Castigate, Defiance, he cannot Castigate himself.
Arcanoi- When the Psyche isdominant, some Arcanoi give a wraith Angst. If the Shadow is dominant, these Arcanoi reduce the Shadow's Angst instead. This does not, however, mean that Pathos or Willpower is gained when the Shadow uses those Arcano1. Only Angst is affected in this manner.
Failing any test - TheShadow cannot stand to see its own failure. For every two tests lost during Catharsis, the Shadow loses one point of Temporary Angst
There is no way known to rnduce Pennanent Angst. BochTemporaryand Pennanenr Angst should be cracked on the Shadow sheet, and it is viral that this be kept current.
Dark passions
Shadows cannot understand the "higher" emotions like love, hope and joy. Jn­ stead, the Shadow only understands the "baser" emotions like hate and anger. Anger and hate can aaso be a Psyche's P.!Sliions, but the Shadow's flavors of rage and loath­ ing tend to be more vicious and less honest.

With this in mind, Shadows have Dark Passions which, when they are in con­ trol, they can fulfill to gain Angst. When a Shadowguide is present, the Shadow can also manipulate his Psyche inco invoking base emotions that feed his Dark Passions in others, as well. Dark Passions for the Shadow are chosen in the same way char Passions are picked for the wraith, and they are phrased in the same way. Sample Dark Passions 111clude:

In any scene where a Shadow or Shadowguided wraith evokes a negative emo­ rion in someone else, the player makes an Extended Simple Test co see if her baser side can sympathize with the emotions of those around her. Only a win in a Simple Te,t will feed the Shadow Angst, though each win gives rhe Shadow another Trait. If a Narrator is present, she must approve the Angst gain by verifying the sincerity of
the emotion felt or invoked. If no Narrator is present, the consent of all players present is necessary for a rest against a Dark Passion.
Drawing Angst from inchoate negativity doesn't always work, an<l it helps if something is focusing the less pleasant emotions. When a wraith {usually in Cathar­ sis, bur also when beingShadowguided) is directly involved in following the dicrates ofh1s Dark Passion, his dark side can gam strength rapidly. In cases where the wmith 1s literally pursuing one of her Dark Passions {in the judgment of a Narrator), an Extended Simple Test allow, the wraith to continue to retest and gam Angst if she ties at any point. Note that continuing co test for Angst is not optional.
Dark Passions, unlike Passions, <lo nor fade if ignored. Dark Pa 1ons can never be Numbed, and cannot lie removed by any means.
Catharsis
henever a Shadow's Temporary Angst reaches 10 points, the
Shadow can attempt to take over the wraith. This 1s called Catharsis. By expending five of its Temporary Angi;t points,
:31 the  wraith's Shadow takes control of their shared Corpus and
·• imprisons the Psyche in a uny comer of its mind. For all in­ tenr.and purpose . the Shadow is the wrnich at that point, and 1t peersout at the world through Oblivion-tintedglasses. How­ ever, the wraith's player still plays her character, even when

--
Oolivion
Shadow-ridden. To the wraith's friends, she will probably appear no different than she did before, and they will probably not know that she is in Catharsis. This allows the Shadow ro do all sorts of vicious things: start rumors, mc1te feuds between Circlemates, plan and carry out actsof treachery, let Spectres m the backdoor, drop favorite relics down Nihils, and generally cut loose with pure malice.
When the Shadow is dominant, it interacts with the world the only way it knows how. It makes useof the Psyche'sArcanoi,gains moreTemporary Angst through irs Dark Passions, and usesany of the Thoms it possesses. It cannot cause the Psyche co regain Pathos, nor can it reduce a character's Pathos or Willpower Traits below J. Usually Catharsis is a threat that the Shadow uses to get its way from time to time. When a Shadow takes control, it can choose to "cut off" a Psyche from his senses, thereby allowing the Shadow perform acts in secret that can later come back to haunt the character.
Catharsis lasts for a single scene or half an hour, whichever comes first. Players are not permitted to end scenesartificially (say, byscurrying out of the room into the hallway for 30 seconds, then returning) just to end Catharsis.
Snadow Bids
heShadow can sometimes help the Psyche out, for 1cs own in· imitable reasons. Sometimes it's to lull the wraith intoa false sense of security, sometimes it's to get the wraith hooked on help that will be pulled away at the last minute, sometimes it's just plain perversity. In any case, there will be times when the Shadow will offer its assi.stance. These moments are marked by Shadow Bids.
To make a Shadow Bid, a player substitutes a Temporary Angst Trait for the Trait chat ought to be bid at the beginning of any challenge or rest. If the wraith loses, the Temporary Angst Trait is lost and the wraith cannot Shadow Bid for an, ocher two hours. lf the wraith wins or ties, he gains an additional Temporary Angst Trait.

Archetypes
Just as each wraith has a face she shows to the world, eachShadow does as well. The Shadow's Archetype combines Nature and Demeanor, as the Shadow has so little chance to demonstrate a false face to the rest of the Underworld. A Shadow's Archerype is at rhe same time its personality and it.'i plan of attack, its way ofdealing with and destroying the Psyche.
There isno rulesapplication for a Shadow Archetype outside of a Harrowing. It merely exists as a roleplaying aid and a way to draw a more complete picture of who your wraith is.

Chaoticand crazed, che Abuser will lash out at any target other than his favorite one: himself.
When dominant, the Abuser will spew his vitriol in all directions. Any excuse will set him off, and if no excuse is provllled, he will create one. Demands for obedi­ ence, absurd requests and systematic verbal or physical abuse are thisShadow's hall­ marks.

Destruction Harrowings
Harrowing is the term for a Harrowing where the target 1s down tu her last pomt of Permanent Corpus. If she lo es, she will dissipate into the Void. There is no difference between a Dcsrruction Harrowing and a regular Harrowing in Oblivion.
As the Narrator in charge of the Harrowing seesonly the Shadow sheet and nor rhe wraith's regular character sheer, she should have no way of knowing whethera Harrowing is DeHruction or ordinary. Leningslip that a Harrowing is Destruction in hopes that the Narrator will "go easy" is extraordinarily bad form, and should result in the instantaneous failure of the Harrowing. The character 1s destroyed, and the playershould create a new one, preferably with more common sense.
Failing a Destruction Harrowing results in the ,bvious: the character's destruc- tion.
justDoing it
When the Harrowing actually starts, the Narrator describes the scene and the other characters present. These other characters are Spectres, bur can he played by additional Narrators or players whose characters are currently out of play. Excessive detail is not required; just enough to set the scene is all that is needed.
Ar thi, point, the Harrowing begins as regulargameplay. All regular rulesapply, though the "reality" of the shared nightmare can sometimes warp in odd ways. How­ ever. wraiths can make challenges, use Arcano1, and otherwise interact normally with this lirrle pocket of unreality. Any Traits bid or used durmg a Harrowing, with the exception of Eidolon, are recovered at rhe Harrowing', end. For purposes of com­ bat within the Harrowmg, the wraith is considered to be at full Temporary Corpus. This is, of course, an 1llus1on, and will fade at the Harrowing's end.

It cannor be emphasized srronglr enough that the reality of the Harrowing is complete. The wrairh muse believe in what isgoing on and ace accordmgly, even i( the wraith's player 1s aware it is a Harrowing. It is
experience bonuses for roleplaying are earned.

Samples
Rescue Me
lng the Harrowed wraith co rescue a loved one, usually from her hvmg
Cna ter ix:1ne)naaow
a Moliated Spectre, but on rareoccasions the actual wraith of the target's loved one) ineven greater danger. The best escape from this sort of Harrowing is for the wraith to accept chat she cannot win and, as difficult as 1t may be, ,he has ro accept fate. Other variations call for the wraith to sacrifice herself for the sakeof the hostage, bur rhis sort of Harrowing is not about triumph. It's abour acceptance.
Dead Day Afternoon - This sort of Harrowing takes the wraith back to his living days, and into the teeth of undeserved peril. The police wish to calk co the wraith, who finds himself fleeing in order to prove his innocence. Every action co facilitate that flight results in more and more illegal acts, mjuring1nnocenc bysrand, er.; and desrroymg property. This of course will convince the police of the wraith's guile. They w1II then step up che chase, and the wraith is forced to choose between escalacion himself or surrender and potential annihilataon.
The lesson here is that the wraith can't do everything himself, and chat by doing so he sometimes does more harm than good. Pocencial escape conditions in, elude either surrendenng ac che risk of destruction, or accepting fully rhe life of the outlaw and all it stands for.
I Have No Mouth and I Must Flee -  The wraith finds herself in che middle of a crowd of people, none of whom will speak co or otherwise interact with her. They will walk past, talk among themselves, laugh, cry and generally carry on with their laves without rhe slightest indication that they are aware of the wraith, ratio, nalize weird or physical effects she creates, and in essence, completely isolate her. Everywhere she goes it is the same; she is completely alone.
The lesson here is chat the wraith should learn chat in the end, her own self is enough and she should not relyon others. Ifshe accepts her owncompany and chooses not to attempt fruitless interaction any longer, instead of givmg up m frustration, she can be considered to have mumphed over chis sort of Harrowing.
Wilderness of Mirrors - The mirror is a favorite psychological image. A funhouse maze, where every pane shows either a distorted image of the Harrowed wraith or one of his enemies, can make for a powerful Harrowmg. Voices coming from everywhere and nowhere, a seemingly infinite supply of mockmg faces, reflec­ tions chat move when the wraith does not - all of these offer tremendous horrific potential.
Mirror-maze Harrowings can be resoh,ed ma variety of different ways. Noc rec­ ommended is the solution when the wraith simply smashes every mirror in sight. lnsread, he might choose to walk into the mirror and join with his disrorted image, gaining a measure of self-acceptance. Another option m1ghr be for che wrairh to find the center of the maze and rest there, dwelling at the heart of all of the images of his self and encompassing all of them. A chm.I idea could have the wraith following rhe trail of the most horribly distorted reflections, descending into the heart of his own darkness and escaping out the other side.

The Missing Hammer -  Try as they might, most Oblivion players will find it impossible nor co realize that they are in a Harrowing and alter their play sryle accordingly. With that in mind, the "missing hammer" technique often works well as a method of keeping players' attention thoroughly in-game.
With the knowledge that she is in a Harrowing, a wraith instantly scare as:;um­ ing the worse. What's more, she instantly stares looking for 1r. If disaster is nor 1mme•
diately forchcommg (thesetting ispleasant, the disguised Spectres are friendly, there's no immediate evidence of torture or ocher unpleasantness), the wraith will seek it 10 every innocent phrase and action, looking for what she feels to be the inevitable
hidden crap. Within a few short minutes, the wraith can make her own Hell, as che Spectres simply act politely and watch the wraith drive herself into a frenzy of para­ noia.
This sort of Harrowing can be difficult co pull off, and should only be used aas contrast to  morestandard psychodramas. The lesson to be learned here is that it is all nghc to accept good things when they come to you, and chat if you lookcoo hard for Hell you just might find it. Wraiths who can actually accept what's going on witha minimum of fuss (there's a difference between acceptance and blind naivete, after all) should be considered to have roleplayed their way from the Harrowing.
Darkness Rising - Through the use of the Dark Arcanos Contaminate, a Spectre can easily take the part of a wraith's Shadow. Bringing a character face to face with his own dark half, someone whocan match his every move and unveil his everysecret, can be extremely unsettling. If the ocher Spectres in the Harrowing are portraying twisted versions of the rest of the wraith's Circle, mockmg and mutating familiar marmerisms, the situationcan gee terrifying. How much of what the Shadow is revealing is being told to the characters and nor to ocher Spectres? How can a wraith defeat hisown darkside?This sort of Harrowingcan also bedoneforsuspense value, with the wraith and his Shadow trapped in a maze or on a desert island, en­ gaged in someson of contest. A variation on "The Most Dangerous Game"can work well, as can gruesome variations on tag.
Again, self-acceptance is a good key to chis kind of Harrowing. A wraith who can embrace his alter ego, no matter how loathsome it might be, and who can inte­ grate it into his own personality, should be able co escape this Harrowing. methods of escape could come from either defeating or escaping the Shadow. On the ocher hand, acceptance is not the same thing as surrender, and any wraith who sim­ ply gives in to his Shadow's blandishments should be considered to have failed rhe Harrowing (a test should still be allowed, but the difficulty should be adjusted up­ ward at Storyteller discretion).
Misplaced Childhood's End-Among themost terrifying things imaginable is innocence gone wrong. When the icons of childhood -  stuffed animals, friendly parental faces, familiar homesteads -  suddenly reveal menace, the impact can be devastating. Returning a wraith to a childhood setting, such as her room in her par• ents' house circa her seventh birthday, and then having evil crawl out of the wood­

work is an effective Harrowing technique. Finding horror in places of safety, and having the images the character most associates with safety taken away from her can really unsettle the underpinnings of a wraith's world. olivion
f characters are the lifeblood of Mind's Eye Theatre, then the chronicle is the body they sustain. Although Oblivion can be used and enJoyed as a series of loosely connected stories, such
games often lacka senseof continuity and offer little opporcu. mty for character development. Furthermore, wraithsoften have long-term goals related to their Passions and Fetters; one story ts rarely enough to resolve anythingsatisfactorily.In a chronicle
-  an ongoing series of stories -  not only do charactel"li reside

in a familiar environment, but one that evolves as they interact with it. Developing and maintaining a chronicle is a satisfying cask, but It requires continuous effort and dedication on the behalf of you and your Narrators.
Setting tneStage
The first element to consider in chronicle construction is the atmosphere you desire. The cwo aspects of atmosphere are moo<l and theme. Each 1s a fundamental ingredient in the alchemy of a chronicle. Although mood and theme have already been discussed in terms of srory, and some ideas previously presented apply here, mood and theme for a chronicle involves ocher aspects.
Mood
Mood is the underlying emotional sentiment of a chronicle. It suHainsand flows through everything you do, unobtrusive yet omnipresent. While the mood of a chronicle is intangible, your choice of mood can be one of the most important ones you make as regards plot, character, and theme. After all, if you're going for a mood of terror, a gunbunny plot (and characters co macch) won't make much sense.
Theme
Themes are central ideasof a chronicle, ones chat help define the shape of the action. They can be as visceral as the struggle for freedom or as intellectual as the age-old questions ofgood and evil.Themes can emerge asseemingly inconsequential encounters or as the major focusof a chronicle.Avoid overemphasizing theme, how­ ever.Overemphasis makesrelated eventsexpecreJ and predictable rather than haunt· ing and prophetic.
Setting
The  setting is the stage upon which the drama of a chronicle b played Sewng is composed of environmenr and locale. Environment describes the overall scheme of the world.The environmenr of Oblivion involves the three levelsofghostly exist· ence, Guilds, Legions, Ferrymen, mortals, PaS51ons, Fetters, Maelstroms and much more. A myriad of potential alternatives are available. It's your reality-do with it what you will.
( apter evteonr:1tellin
Locale, or the physical point where you choose to place your chronicle, is the next step coward chronicle creation. The easiest choice for chronicle setting is the very ciry in which you and your players live. During your games, you can visit and utilize parts of the city in which your stories cake place. lr's also a good bet that your players are familiar enough with their setting co incorporate all sorts of firsthand knowledge into their play.
If you don't chink your home cown would do as your chronicle's locale, or sim­ ply don't want to use your actual location, there's a whole world out there. Book­ store,, libraries and courm bureaus can provide you with enough detailed informa­ tion to set a chronicle in almost any city. This sort of research is also helpful if your players move around a great deal in the chronicle. Using foreign locales takes a bit more imagination, but if you're playing this game, you're probably nor running low in thar department anyway.
When it comes down co it, who says your chronicle muse be sec in the modem da ·? Although we suggest other times only forexperienced players and Storytellers, a number of historical eras provide excellent settings for chronicles.

Creating t eCicy
The c1cy is the locus of yourchronicle. You need to present your city in a realis­ tic and consistent manner so that players believe in it. The setting must live and breathe for players, and, until it does, characters cannot come co be. The city is 'Vhcre your stories take place - the more interesttng and exotic 1t seems, the more Your stories come to life. With each story you run, define the c1ry a little more, but don't give away everything at once.
As the chronicle progresses, add details of geography, cusrom and plot. Build the city bit by bit at whatever pace you choose. The mortal half of the city isfairly

Castingtne parts
Once you know what is in your city, it's time to rhank about who. Both morcal, and wraiths should be your concern here; neither side is terribly effective without the other. It's generally best to Starr at the top -  who are the Anacreons, the local Guildmasters, the leaders of Gangs and Cults in the area. Deathlords and Ferrymen should be used sparingly, if at all; they're simply too powerful. You should also think about whether any Spectres have infiltrated your Necropolis. On the mortal side, political leaders and ghost-hunters are equally important. If there is another super­ natural presence m your city, you will wane to detail that as well. The local vampire and werewolf populations, and their relations to the Restless, are all important things to have mapped out in advance.
intrigue
Intrigue is a vital ingredient of your chronicle. For people co play our intrigue, they need to be drawn into it. Power is often a lure. This power needs to be immedi­ ate and real. It needs to be visible and obtainable. It needs co be given out only occasionally co inspire characters to pursue it, but not so often that opportunities are passed on.
Ambition
Many characters eventually wanr to assume positions of power in the c1cy. In some games, this is Just another aspecr of the self-generating plots players contrive on their own. In ocher games, you may plan che characters'search for power from the begmnmg. In either case, you need to learn what long-term goals your players wane to accomplish lo the chronicle. You can then tailor an occasional session co the,e goals, hue don't give the players everything they wane. Some may covet powerful positions in the Hierarchy, or co advance the political agendas of outs1der grou they belong co.Othersmayseek Transcendence or knowledge of the missing Charon. Both goals are power in one form or another, both over others and over one's self.

More than a few players don't know or admit what they want in the long-term, or may not Jeme power. Don't worry, chey'll sec cheir sights on something in time, especially 1£ cempced by their ShaJows or other players. Practice srringing players along. Keep a carrot dangling before them. The price of power
In most chronicles, ,r's likely that one or more characters are in a position of power. If chis is the case, the players need to know exactly what their responsibilities and advantages are. In general, any players in the role of Anacreon, high-ranking Guild member, Heretic or Renegade leaderor Ferrymanshould beexperienced enough 10 assist you as a Narrator. As powerful characters can have rremendous impact on
lesi;er characters and the story as a whole, players should know what the ramifica­
tionsof their acnons are and must take them intoconsideration.Only experience in play develops this insight, but it 1s viral that players with characters in positions of power understand both their responsibilities and their limits. Powerful characters, if abused, can serve to discourage players with weaker characters. This drives off new players, and evenrually the power-gamers may find themselves with no one to lord it over except one another.
chronicle Dynamics
The  key to a successful chronicle 1sa constant and uninterrupted flow ofaccion. In the real world, events never stopoccurring, and everyone, in one way or another, gets dragged into life's chaos. Your chronicle should have the same power. All char­ acters should be drawn mco the events around them. Every character should be do­ ing something. Those in conflict may bide their rime and pause to marshal their
forces, but the tension never recedes. After all, if you give your players time to rest,
relax and think, they may think about the fact that they're just playing a game. Too much time for reflection can shatter the most carefully crafted illusionary worlds.
Trouole inall shapes anctSizes
There are dozens of thmgs in a story that cango wrong. This is parricularly true of a chronicle. With the added level of complexity that comes with a chronicle, problems with continuity, advancement, numerous players, constant change and background plots are inevitable. A few of the more common chronicle problems, and how to deal with them, are derailed below:
Problem Players - This problem is probably one of the most delicate and potentially disastrous you can face. For one reason or another, one or more players are disrupting the story and ruining everyone's good time. This can be the result of many things: cheating, rule quotation ("rule lawyering"), personal vendettas or im­ proper behavior.
Once you·detect a problem, your first action should be coapproach the players privately and explain what they are doing and why you don't like 1r. At chat point, most people attempt cochange their ways... and that's char. Unfortunately,some are prone co backsliding or just don't take the problem seriously, disregarding your warn­ ing. The next step to consider is somesort of penalty.Temporary suspension from the

•:
Onlivion
chronicle is usually effective. Jf players continue to make trouble, you may have to perform the distasteful rask of barring them encirely from your chronicle.
Do not be afraid to desrroy characters that unbalance your game. If the character's player i worthwhile, he'll understand the need for the sakeof all of the other players.
Favoritism - There isoften a tendency to give friendsspecial treacmenc. Be vigilant to this habit m all those who run and play the game, including yourself. If the problem does arise, try to remember that this is Just a form of entertainment; no one can rea:;onably hold you responsible for a character's loss. If the problem contin­ ues, cry to isolate problem individuals from encounters where favorinsm might oc­ cur. As a final option, remove the offender from any position in which she can dis­ pense favors.
Grudge - ln this situation, an individual is created unfairly for some reason that 1s not related ro her character's nature or actions. Such is often the case with players who are not fond of each other in the real world. Handle tt m a manner similar to favoritism problems.
Stagnation - Even the most imaginative Storyteller occasionally rum out of ideas. Perhaps you're running the game too often to give yourself time to create new and original stories. If this is the case, consider spacing sessions further apart to pro­ vide yourself more planning time. Another solution is to takeon moreStorytellers or Narrators ro give the chronicle a greater creative base on which to draw. A plot coordination council ofsomesort1salmost essential to running large-scale chronicles.
Logistics-  Problems with supplies and locations are bound to arise. Jf this is the case, be sure to communicate with everybody involved in the game. Players are
often able to help procure a setting, props, incidental supplies and all manner of other needs.
Getting the News Out -Sometimes you just don't have enough people to play the type of chronicle you've planned. This is moreoften a case of poor aJvertfa­ mgthan genuine lack of bodies. Consider posting notices on electronic bulletin boards or your local university network, m game and hobby stores, ar conventions, at gam• ing organizatioru;, with theatre or drama groups, on coUege campuses and anywhere else you think imaginative people with a taste for the original might frequent.
Staying Focused - At times your story may drift away from its original plot, or players may become lost. They may become too wrapped up in thetr own subplot> or may be unable ro unravel a puzzle. When this happens, you need something co brmg the story back on course.
Having a few extra props available can be helpful when the story goes off on a tangent. Many of the prop idea-; already suggested can be used to reorient players, For example, an answering machine audio tape with an important message can renew char· acters' interest in their original goal. Documents concaming important information are another option, allowing characters a new avenue in pursuit of the tr original goal.

in malls,
Obviously, extreme care must be taken when using these sites. You must ensure that all of the players are courteous and unobtrusive when playing in a public place. Somesitesare more conducive to certain rypesof stories. You mayfinda particular location is your favorite, but even then, you should keep your mind open to change. A change of locale,even if foronly one scene, can help revive a dying
Most locations (or "sets") can be decorated to convey a	mood. You must
seek out sites that cater to the style of your game and nature of its plot. For best results, thesettingshould havea basis in reality. Inother words, if it'sa highenergysocial aono•

tual setting, perhaps a museum or art gallery is appropriate. When choosing a location, look foran area that establishes the mood and aonosphere you're after.

Music: Music can be an 1megral part of establishing rhe mood of a story. It
lendsa hand in creating drama and, when appropriate, tension. When planning your story be certain to select music chat complements your intended mood. Also make sure you have a variety of music on hand in case the mood changes. However, take care not to let the music get obtrusive or too familiar. lf your players have to shout over thundering lyrics, or 1( your ghosts find themselves singing along to your soundtrack, your game will rapidly dissolve inro chaos.
Audio Effects: Above and beyond music, certain sounds and effects can en­ hancean evening. Horror and sound effect CDs, found in any music score, are a good place to start. A goo<l CD can provide a variety of sound effects to simulate many different events, like tires screeching, gunshots or barking dogs. Proper timing of these sounds 1s critical. If you're able to pre-mix a tape of effects, you can play them at appropriate moments in the story. Preparation is the key. Have your effects cued
up ahead of time so chat, wich the mere touch of a button, hideous screams can he
heard by all. Preparation makes for more drama than having to delay the game while you set up the right sound.
Lighting Effects: The Underworld is, not surprisingly, a place of dim lights and many shadows. With a minimum of effort, you can reproduce chis sort of effect for your game of Oblivion. Remember, a few well-placed shadows can hide a great many anomalies that might orherwise distract your players from the game.
Avoid high wattages and white light. Multicolored light bulbs have the broad­ est effect. By arranging multicolored light bulb, in different parts of the room, you can create shadowy areas and still have well-ht "white light" areas where the colors intersect. If you have track lighting, recessed multicolored bulbs work very well. If you can't get multicolored bulb., cloth draped over lights works iust as well (bur avoid putting cloth in direct contact with hot bulbs). The key is contrast from low wattage bulbs. Keeping enough low lights to contrast with one another results in plenty of light to see byand plenty of shadows to hide in. Play around with different combinations to determine what works best for your mood and scene.

Whtie candles may be very atmospheric, they should be used extremely spar• ingly. Recreating the desolation of the Shadowlands in theory is a good chmg, but Jomg o by bummgdown your site generally has a detrimental effect on a chronicle. Even without candles, though, if you keep your wattage low, avoid direct white light, and experiment w1ch your color schemes, you should be able co find the right com bi· nation to create the proper atmosphere for your story.
props
After you've got a story, players, a place to play and even selected a cool soundtrack to accompany the game, something else is still needed co bring the scary to life. This is the poinr at which props come in. Props for a story don't need co be grandiose or expensive. Mose props can be found m your own home or your grand· parents' attic.
In most forms of theatre, pror, play an extensive role. In fact, in modem movie,,, props can at rimes overwhelm the viewer, sometimes derractmg from the ftlm. ln this game, propsshould be kept relatively s11nple andshould nor attract more anention than the srory itself. blanks.Since thi;,1sa scorytdlmg game, unaginatmn is of the ucmo,,c1mpor1.ance. This ts not cosay that you should besparing with props. If you have the available maccnal , go wild. Just make sure char the props are not the focus of the story. The spotlight shoulc..l
always remain on the characters, not on what surrounds them.
General props
General propsare items thatcan help you further a story bygiving playerssome• thing that they can physically examine. The Storyteller should be wary of how often physical props are used and what tmpact they haveon the story. If they are allowed to dominate a story, players may begin to rely on them rather than interacting with one another. Besure co inform players when props will be used in a story. Otherwise, they are likely to disregard an important clue, considering it just another feature of the
place you're playing in.
Many items can be u ed as general props to help enhance your scory. Books, jewelry, documents and pieces of artwork are a few examples. These props can be used to actually give information, or they can merely give ms1ghc inco the character of che person possessing the prop. Somerimes an item has to be represented by an item card, as in the case of weaports. Also, a card may be discreetly attached to an item, giving more information to a player who mspects the item.
Creative and effective usage of general props can make a story more interesting for everyone, particularly if props arc used to represent Fetter:, with plot (as well as character) significance. The important thmg is costay focused and avoid overloaJ­ ing yourself with them. If you come up with an idea for a prop, but are unable to  implement it, don't panic -  your story can stand on its own. After all, people, not

things, make a story.
personalprops
Personal props, like costumes, are used by players to help d1Hingu1sh them as their characters. These propscan 1:-e particularly useful for Narrator characters, espe• cially when Narrators must often change from character co character.
Many different items can be used to help portray a character. Cloaks, jackets, scarves, hats, canes and jewelry are only a few of the accessories chat can give a character a distincnve look. You may want to prov1Je certain items to players to aid them in chis manner, especially if players are uncertain of the characters they are playing. For example, if you plan a story involvmg a Renegade gang, you may supply players with matching bandannas to identify them as gang members.
Many personal props can be found right m your own home. Searching through the attics of relatives can reveal old clothing and Jewelry. Costume jewelry works best; real jewels can be far too valuable to lend out for a game. Should all else fail, second-hand stores can be great places to pick up a few last-minute props at an af­ fordable price. All manner of clothing, incluc..ling hars, canes anJ CDlitume jewelry, can be acquired at such emporiums.
In many stones, not much is needed by way of personal props. Sometimes, only a cane or piece of jewelry can make a character. The most important thing to re• member is to have fun with props, avoiding dangerous ones.

No-Nos
Weapons should never be used as props. In addition, props must be understood to be someone's property, not some character's properry. If your players start taking home the props that belong to their characters, perhaps it's time to reconsider using props at all.If you chose to go this route,cards (like those used to represent weapons) are best.
CreatingStories
Story creation can be as simple or as detailed as you want. The more effon you put intopreparing astory, the smoother it runs when it'sexecuted. There is nosmall amount of satisfaction to be gained from writing a successful and appreciated story. Finding the right combination of stimuli to ignite creative thought can be diffi­
cult. For some of us, creativity comes naturally. For others, it's more difficult cobring
creative aspects of personality to the fore. However, this isexactly what you must do in order to create a good story. Creativity is not generally taught in school. In fact, it'soften discouraged. Rediscovering your childhood enthusiasm is one way to find a wellspring of imagination.
Inspiration can come from any source. Magazines, movies, books, theatre, tele­ vision, friends, family... you get the picture. The challenge is to take what you see and change it into something all your own. Inspiration can be, quite literally, any stimulus that ignites the process of creative thought. Hence, inspiration is veryoften as dependent on what's without as on what's within. What sends one person's mind racing may bore someone else. You must find the things that inspire you (if you don't already know) and associate with them in order to stoke your creative fires.

plot
A story's plot is its progression of events. Plot not only mvolves the machina­ tions of the powers that be, but the machinations of the characters as they pursue their own goals in the story. Plots are essential to the successful progression of the story. They serve as a beginning to, and a guide during, the story.
A story can haveany number of plots (see "Story Style," below,for moredetailson this).These plotsshould all be wound together withskill and care before the actual game begins. The trick is o have all the pieces of a plot already wrinen m the characters' briefings so they can read about their own respective pans of the overall plot ac the game's beginning. Together, the characters' backgrounds compose the main story, and by pursumg their own pans of the story, characrers progress the plot as a whole.
During a story, it's often helpful to keep a schedule of events. This is a pre• planned sequence of events (usually initiated by outside forces) thac'sgoing to occur regardless of the characters' accions. Typically, characters played by Narrators keep the schedule on track. It is also helpful to integrate occasional Narrator characters into rhestory to assist the progression of the plot (or plots). These tacticsshould be used sparingly; otherwise players may grow dependent upon interaction with Narra­ tors instead of with each other. However, occasional Narrator intervention can add a new level of unpredictability and excitement to a story.
ElementsofaStory
The elements of a story are best defined by its components or stages. It isoften helpful to think of plot elements in renns of tense (past, present and future). In terrru; of story structure, these elements are the addressed in the opening, climax and reso­ lution. At the beginning of the scory, characters reflect on events of their past that have brought them to thestory.The story's climax is where the story's plot (or plots) are exposed and dealt with bysome or all of the characters. Finally, there's the reso­ lution, where the story comes to a close and hooks for future stories can be laid.
Opening
The  opening can be any point from which you choose to begin yourstory. It can be a gradual, gentle start or a shocking plunge into the world of the Restless. The latter works best if the characters start out as living humans; the former is better for extended chronicles with older wraiths.
At the beginning of a story, each character should have a written synopsis of
recent events that"everyone" would be aware of. This provides a basisfor your plots, as well as layingdown somecommon ground for characters to discuss. ("Did you hear about that Legionnaire whogot cashiered for taking bribes?""Yeah, real shame about that. Hecut me loose a couple of times  "andsoon). Furthermore, by establishing
common knowledge, you're also helping to set the tone and theme of your game.
The synopses of common knowledge and recent personal evenrsshould be brief explanations of the situation at hand. They should be precise and intriguing. These synopses should be the first media through which players get a feel for the story's mood. The synopses should also hint at the story's main theme. You may even wish to foreshadow the plot, but that depends on your personal style.

Climax
When the main plot is on the verge of conclusion, the story is considered at irs cli­ max.This usually happenswhen characters havediscovered all they need to know cosolve whatever problem faces them and are on the verge of resolving the story. The tension b usually at its highest point at tlus time. If the operung wassraged carefully, and the players arecooperative and imaginative, the climaxcan bereached with linleadditional effort on
thepart of Narrators. However, even the bestof planscan be mislaid, or perhaps the Story­ teller and Narracors prefer to takea moreactive role in the progression of your story's plot. Eitherway, thereare times whena Narrator's intervention iswarranted. The climaxshould be the high point of the story, something the players should always anticipate and work toward However, thiselement should never be rushed. It'sa matter of riming.
Extended chromcles shouldn't have everything wrapped up in a single climax.
After all, you need to have something for the Restless to do next week.
esolution
The story's resolution is where all (or most) of the pieces come together. The resolution should be used as a means of winding the excitemenl down while cultivat­ ing an interest in future stories. Players should always end a story feeling as though

Cast
Another factor to take mtocoruidcration is the type of characters cast members romay. The best way co determine this is to ask them. A story usually works best if players are enacting characters they are comfortable with, although ir can occasion­ ally b<!ihterestmg to cast players in roles different from what they're used m playing.

This variation in casting should only be done if you think it would be enjoyahle for all the players.
An important consideration is whether you are g1vmg players pre-made charac­ ters, with complete backgrounds, Attribut andArcanoi, or tf players are allowed to make their own characters. Sometimes the guidelines lie somewhere m-hetween.
Obviously, the easiest of these opuons 1s to use pre-made characters. This allows you ro customize your story to the characters, tying them to one another to make for a more mmguing plot. The problem with cll!l, is that casr memhers are sometimes disappointed with the characters they gee. This can detract from everyone's cnJOY· menr.
Story Sfyle
Style is the method you use in constructing your story. It describes how the subplots tic m with the main plot and how charncters interact with the environment you have chosen. Style is the thread from which your story is woven. Without style, a stor)' has no cohesive form and mighc become bormg for players. You need to de­ velop and hone your style so as to be able to craft a finely tuned story, with many layers and intricacies for players to explore.
Main plot
The main plot, or metaplot, is the root of a story, and any other lesser plots will grow from the main plot. The main ploc is what bnngscharacters rogerher, unifymg their attention. The main plot of a story should be all-encompassmg, in that all characters should be affected by it in at least a minor fashion. The main plot should illuminate every other aspect of the story. Not all of them need be touched by it direcrly, bur each character should be aware of che main plot ac least on some periph­ eral level.
Su plots
In most novelsand movies,subplotsare just filler material. They aree sential ro Mind's Eye Theatre. Subplots keepcharacren, busy and carry them from momenc to moment. 1l1ey are the stones chat are woven around the characters. A story gives players ample opporrunities to exploresubplots, and due to the unique nature of this son of game, subplots can be taken as far as the players want to take them.
Each character should be involved m as many subplots as possible. A suhplot can be any personal goal a character is crying to accompli,h. Subplots should be crafted so as to intermingle with one another, so tha1 characters become embroiled in a weh of intrigue.

The more layersof intrigue in a story, the more players enJoy untangling them. Indeed, if your scning is intricate enough, you may find players developing subplots of their own, adding to the confusion. This should be encouraged, because 1t helps the story grow and gives it a life of ics own.
A subplotcan sometimes be almost as large in scope as the main plot, the differ­ ence being that it isnornecessary for everyone to be involved. However, you should take care that the subplot does not override the main plor.

C aracter elations
Character interaction isa part of everystory and every chronicle. Intricatechar­ acter interaction is essential to a story. That is not to say that all characters will gee along harmoniously - character strife is usually much more interesting. However, making sure that characters relate to one another requires a bit of planning on your part. Making sure that players create backgrounds for their characters helps, as it gives you more material ro work with to create common ground for wraiths.Spread­ ing rumors that provide incentive for discussion, trade, and cooperation is another good way to go, as is the inclusion of a nasty villain who will forcecharacters at least to discuss working together.
MultipleGoals
Each of us possesses a multitude of goals and ambitions. Many of them conflict, and we have to choose between them. For instance, you might want to go to the movies on the sameday you want to visita friend. Multiple goalscomplicate matters, and while in real life that can be bothersome, in thisgame it only adds to rhe excite• menc. The more complicated you can make things, rhe better.
Complications such as multiple goals give characters depth and keep players b -y. A player with several goals is usually occupied throughout the course of a story and is rarely bored. Indeed, if players with multiple goals achieve even one of those goals, they probably leave the 5tory with a sense of accomplishment.
Narration andStorytelling
ow that you have written a story, you need to make it work in a livegame. Narratinga story is an art akin co jugghng; you have to keep upwith allstory threads, play judgeand makesure that none of the players get bored - you'll be juggling quite a few of them. Narration requires a lot of work and planning, and usually leaves youexhausted. You, however, havea ringsideseat cothe storyand can watch it unfold in its entirety. Because your control of the story is loose (tight conttol denies player freedom), you may be
surprised by how the story cwisrs and changes in the handsof your players. If youdo your
job well, you will be rewarded with the players' thanks.
Of course, with the great rewards of storytelling comes a great deal of responsi• bilicy. You have to initiate and guide the story and present it in an entertaining manner. You, aboveall people involved, can destroy the story. Evena verygoodsrory ca n be ruined if it's not presented well. If a few required player handoucs are missmg,

(l1apter e\·en: torrtellin
or if players have no idea where the game is taking place, you have failed. It is very important that you prepare every aspect of chat story as far ahead of time as possible. Jf you try to prepare an entire game at the last minute, you'll be stressed out hy the time the game begins. The game will suffer heavily from your exhaustion.
Veteran roleplaying Storyrellers should take note char runrung a live game is very different from running old-style roleplaying games. You can no longer sit down

an hour before play commences and dream up a quick plot. If you try ro do this, your story will have lots of holes. If you change your scrcing in mid-story, you need co represent the change in some wayso players realize where they are without having co
ask. Prepare everything in advance, or suffer the folly of sloth.
The main rule co remember when preparing your story is "show, don't tell." You have to prcsenr your scoryso players can make their own impressions of the environ­ menr without your impressions beingforced on them. Livegames work because play­ erscan take the lead based on information they discover themselves. If you have not fleshed out your srory enough - if you have co cell players what's going on - you might as well be playinga tabletop rolcplaying game.
To be a Storyteller, you need to work magic. You have to create the illusion that your playersare ghosts, dwellingso close to the "real" world but unable to be a part of ic. Fortunately for you, your players will probably cooperate in every way to help you maintain the illusion, but it's up to you to direct their attention from the gamelike aspects of roleplaying.
How toHost aGame
Running an entirechronicle can be exhausting unless you pace yourself. Expect to spend a mimmum of an hour per player writing the story and setting everything up. Expect to acrually play a story bi-monthly or even monthly. Trying co run a weekly game is very taxing and may result in the eventual death of your chronicle. Don't make your games frequent and tedious, but rare and spectacular.
Storyteller focus
As a Storyteller, your focus should always be on keeping the game runnmg as smoothly as possible. To ensure that the game doesn't get out of hand, make sure you have enough Narrators. They'll serve to answer players' questions and resolve che majority of inter-player disputes, as your arbitration should only be required in the most extreme circumstances.
More importantly, though, your Narrators will be an mcegral part of game ac­ tion.Serving asShadowguides and Harrowing Masters, Narrators will be in constant imeraction with players in all levels of your game. This requires a greacdeal of com­ mitment from your Narrators, as they'll constantly be on the go, interacting with only a few characters at a time. On the up side, by having your Narrarors serving as Shadows, you have an instant conduit for getting information and rumors to charac­ ters, as well as a constant source of player feedback on what's going on. Rather than having to cook up a convoluted plot to let characters know that an Anacreon's gone corrupt, you can just have a Shadow whisper it in a wraith's ear. At the same time, if the action slows or people are gening frustrated, having your Narrators constantly on the floor will enable you to pinpoint trouble spots quickly.

Concentrate on where players stand in terms of resolving the plot or solving the mystery you have given them, lf they are struggling, drop a few hints and spread a few rumori,, then watch your Restless scramble to discover new information. If they are too close to solving the mystery, throw in a red herring or create a new scene on rhe fly. Improvisation is a potent arr, and Storytellers who learn to use it are well-rewarded.

If you are playing in public, you must serve as a buffer between the authoritiesof the real worlJ and the pocemial mayhem players can create. Discretion 1s the better part of valor. Educate players on responsible playing, and do not hesitate to hand out rewards or punishments as the need arises.
Preparation is essential to running a successful story. The greater the preparation, the l you have co worry about maintaining focus. A well-prepared game frees you co enJoy your creanon and interact with players, while a poorly prepared game leaves you scrambling to maintain control. When you mamcain focus on story elements, the sur­ roundings and your players, you need not fear the results of any game session.

Guiding players
As your players begin to play out the story, it's possible that they may be unsure of themselves. New players in particular need some sort of guidance to help them learn the rulesand get used to acting out their characters' actions. Stepintoa minor character of your story and engage a new player in a challenge to get her used to the rules. The challenge system works well in play, but most people do nor immediately understand it until they actually defend themselves in a challenge. Try to let new players win your challenge, as it builds their confidence, and confidence is sorely needed when newcomers play in the same game as more experienced players.
Sometimes cast members also need help with their goals. A story that leans heavily toward investigation may frustrate players who miss one or two vital clues. Eventually, some players may come over to you asking for help. Unless it is a rules question, or you have to clarify some point that a character should know, you might not want to give information away too easily at first. If a player receives immediate assistance from a Storyteller or Narrator, you introduce an omnipresent bemg into the game. Players may become dependent on you.
Instead of giving direct artSWers to player questions, encourage players to figure things our for themselves. Later, if a player really does need help, have another Nar­ rator, in the guise of a minor character, assist the player. If another Narrator is not available, you can approach the player and offer aid through your own character (although having another Narrator do it involves a touch of finesse). Pull your assis, rance off cleanly, and the player will never know you have responded to his plea.
You also need to invent things to do for players who seem bored with the game. These players have usually accomplished or hopelessly failed their goals,or can't find rhe motivation ro pursue a difficult goal. Your bestopl.ion is to get a bored player involved in another plot, or to make upa customized plot on the spot. If your character mentions a lead to a powerful Arrifucc that ochersare looking for, the player mayspend time follow­ ing that lead. Alternatively, giving the bored player a clue that someone else has missed allows you to kill cwo birds wirh one stone. It'sa good idea to havea library of new plots on hand that you can drop into your chronicle as needed.
Remember to be vigilant for bored players during your first few games; first-time players might not come back if they're bored all evening long. If you don't keep the

interacting withCastMembers

Your key co interacting with cast members is the minor character known as an extra. An extra can be a pre-generated character already woven into the story, or a character created on the spur of the moment to fix a problem. You rarely need to create a character sheet for your extra; a basic concept is usually enough.
Each character you introduce needs to have an easily identifiable mark so play­ ers can recognize which minor character you are playing. A wearable prop, such as a hat, coat, scarf, bandanna or amulet is usually enough. A distinct speech pattern, like an accent, is at least as useful. Remember that the mark needs to be somethmg that you can put on and take off quickly, since you may end up having to switch between several minor characters in an evening's story.
Your excra(s) should be enjoyable to play. During the middle of a story, you may find that you and the Narrators have little to do. You then have a chance to play your bit parts just for fun. Go a little wild. Try to provide some comic relief.
playerQuestions
Aside from baving a way to deal wid1 players within the context of the story, you
need to handle players' questions outside the story. During the first half-hour of a game, it's a good idea to keep a Narrator "out of game" to answer any questions players might have. When players step out of character to askquestions, you don't want them in the
main playingarea. Set me "outside" Narrator up in anomer room. The most important ming to remember when interacting wim players is to never remind mem mey are play­ ing a game; addressing a Narrator as a Narrator does ju.5t mac.
You may occasionally need to impart information mat a character cannot offer. You have to provide such information as discreetly as possible wimout distracting players from meir characters. Shadows are me besr way to go about doing this; re­ member that they may haveaccess to the Spectres' hive mind if you need to explain where me info came from.
If you want co be srylish about offering game information to players, you can prepare notes for Narrators to hand to players who discover mings. You can also put notes in closed envelopes where clues might be found. You can write requirements on the outside an envelope, requirements that must be met before me player may look at me clue within.
One ming you want to avoid whendealing with players is letting mem overuse Narrators. Players may try to use Narrators as messengers, cosummon other charac­ ters, or to try new and interesting abuses of Arcanoi. If a player asks for such favors too often, Narrators can simply refuse their help. Especially in Oblivion, with che added tasks of Shadowguiding and running 1:-larrowings, Narrators are simply m too short a supply to allow them to become hostages to characters or roles.

Good uses of the plot hammer include killing off problem characters, introduc­ in>,:a powerful outsider who'll forcecharacters to cease pointless internal squabbling, bringing in an overwhelming threat that dictates that everyone work together to srop it, or, in a last resort, stopping the game until you've had time to regroup and your player., have had a chance co calm down. ngDown tne w
It's your responsibility to ensure that players are not a hazard to others, includ­ ing people who are not involved in the game. It is mandatory that the normal rules of social interaction be adhered to strictly. Most players will probably work with you in this regard. However, from nme to time, there are those who prove disrupuve to other players and rhe environment where the game 1s being played.
Many ttmes thisdisruption occurs by accident. However, there may be instances when players get out of hand on pu . If such a s1ruation presents itself, it's not considered bad taste co remove the offender from your game. It is, after all, yourgame and your creation. The integnty of your game should be preserved for the enjoyment of other players. The integrity of the game should also be upheld m the minus of those whodo not play or understand Mind's Eye Theatre.

esolving uments
AsStoryteller (anJ to a lesser degree a, a Narrator), ynu have final word in any dispute that may arise between players. IL 1s your rc:;ponsihilicy to maintain peace between players to insure the :.mooth !low of the scory and its enjoyment by all.
Players shoulJ be given the opportunity to speak their mind\ on a situation in which they Jisagree. While it ts suggested rhat you listen to a player's grievances, reml!mber that your decision is final and muse he adhered to by all concerned. It's besc to combmc an understanding car anJ a firm hanJ. Keep your wm about you, and your players will respect you for,r. lfa player's problems prove a disruption Lothe story, they may have to be put on hokl and dealt with after rhe night's session.
Breakingthe ules
The rules are yours to use and abuse as you see fit. The novelist Richard Bach once smd, "Argue for -,our limiumons, and sure enough, tlu.ry're -,ours." Do nor limit yourself to this set of rules. Your concept of the way Oblivion should be played may change constantly. Bend or break the rules to your liking. The rulesserve the story; the story does not serve the rules. If you come upon a situation that isn't covered by the rules, don't panic - improvise. lmprovi ing rules involves applying common sense to a situation. Don't be afraid of going against the grain in such cases. By playing this game, you are exploring a new frontier in roleplaying. It is your duty ro
rest the limits of Mind's Eye Theatre.

fascinate
Fascinate is the ability ofsome Risen to affect the minds of the Quick by msen­ ing some thought or compulsion that the person cannot banish. It issimilar to Keen­ ing, although it works with simple thoughts instead of pure emouon. This ability is frequently used when a Risen's Passions can be fulfilled by coercing a mortal into perfonning some action he might be already inclined to take.
The use of Fascinate is a very intimate interaction with a mortal. To use Fasci­ nate, che Risen must make eyecontact with the target, and the abilicy is easier to use if the two are not alone. Also, Fascinate will not cause the mortal ro do anything contrary to his Nature. Instead it is a tool of persuasion and manipulation, wearing on the target's nerves and will until he complies.

Deja Vu: Most of us are surrounded by the faces of strangers, many of which never wonn their way mtoour recollection. By using thisability, the Risencan make a person remember having seen her before, regardless of whether this is true or not. To use Deja Vu, the player makes a Social Challenge against the target. Sparkof Deathforthe  sen
hile the character creation process for a Risen is mosdy identical to that for a wraith, there are a few differences tacked onro the end. Risen do not get Backgrounds, nor do they get Influences.
On a slightly different level, there is the question of ap­ pearance. The Risen are physical beings, and their appearance is tied co their body. The wounds that killed a Risen will ap­ pear asscars on her body. A player who is taking a Risen char­
acter needs to decide if others will be able to sec the scars of her death or whether they are hidden from view. She also need to decide what her Risen character looks like: ls she still wearing the half-decomposed clothes that she was buried in1Is her skin a light-blue/whitish tint from having embalming fluid in her veins and being buried for a while? All these appearances are important coallow roleplaying to flour­ ish between other players and yourself.
Becoming sen
Existing wraith characters may choose, with Storyteller permission, to become Risen. Assuming the wraith meets all of the Arcanos requ1temenrs, she can petition a Storyteller to become Risen. If her character's body is still more or less intact, and she canconvince herShadow (played bya Narrator) that crossing the Shroud would be a good idea, the only thing standing in the way of Rising 1s a Static Physical Challenge, along with the investment of two Pathos and one Willpower Trait.
Mind you, the Shadow will dnve a hard bargain for its cooperation, anythmg from beingguaranteed "frolic" time to the desrruccion of a single Fetter. While most Shadows would love the opportunity to do damage in the Skinlands, they will play hard co ger and attempt to wring as many concessions as possible from the Psyche
before relentmg.

Characters may expend Influence Traits co accomplil.h goals relating to a specific
aspecr of mortal society. Many Influences, such as Street and Underworld, perform Simi­ lar functions, but generally one will be more efficient at performing the task m que5tion. Set by the ScoryteUe.r, the difficulty of a cask equals the number of Traits char must be
expended to accomplish the cask, and can besubject tO sudden change depending upon circumstance.The suggestedguideline listedalongwitheach area ofInfluence can change dramatically between chronic! oreven between sessions.
Sometimes a Narrator will require a challenge of some sort to represent the uncertainty or added difficulty involved when exercising Influence. Some uses of Influence may not acruaHy cost Influence rouse, but rather require that the player simply possess a certain level of the Influence in question.
To use Influence actively, you should explain to the Narrator what sort effect you wish to create.The Narrator then decides the Trait cost, the time involved (both real and m-game) and any tests required to achieve the Influence effect. Influence Traits used this way are temporarily considered tO have been expended, and are nor recovered until che next session. The effects of using Influence can be instantaneous and brief, or slow to manifest and permanent, depending on the nature of the ma­ nipulation and the degree of power the character wields.
Somerimes players will want to perform actions that do norseem to fall under any smgle Influence group, or which may require the use of multiple Influences. Say a character wants to open a nightclub in her city. Obviously, she will need Finance, but High Society and Media might be useful co stimulate interest and promote the
club as something really spectacular. Furthermore, what if the area targeted for her venture is not zoned for clubs? Bureaucracy could be useful in changing zoning, not to mention in acquiring the proper licenses and permits. Therefore, in certain cases, a Storyteller may decide that rwo or more types oflnfluence are necessary to accom­
plish a goal. Not only is this more realistic, but it alsoencourages characters to inter­ act more widely than they might have otherwise in order to obtain the Influences they need.
Humans can trade Influences witheach orher much like children trade baseball (or Arcadia) cards. These trades may be permanent or temporary. ln the case of permanent trades, the old owner erases the Trait from his sheet and turns over the appropriate Influence card (if your chronicle uses these) to the new owner. The new owner then records her newly acquired Influence Trait on her character sheet. Tem­ porary tradesof Influence occur whena human is merely doinga favor or loaning her Influence to someone else. In lhis case, the owner does not erase the Trait, but in­ stead makes a note chat it 1s no longer in her possession. The holder of the Influence Traic may use Lt immediately or hold onto to 1t until she feels she needs it. However, the original owner of the Influence Trait may no, regain the Trait until the current holder expends or voluntarily returns it.

Sometimes characters may wish to cry to counteract the Influence of other char­ acters. In such c es, irgenerally costsone Trait per Trait bemg countered.The char­ acter willing to expend the most Influence Traits (assuming she has them lO spend) achieves her goal; all Traits used in chis sort of conflict are considered expended.
In practice, the use of Influence is never instantaneous and rarely expedient. While a character may be able to, say, condemn any building in the city, 1t will nor be tom down that night. For sake of game flow, a Storyteller may allow trivial uses of
225
onlivion
Influence co only take half an hour. Major man1pularions, on the other hand, can become the cenrer of ongoing plots requiring several sessions to bring to fruition.
The guidelines below by no means limit the number of Influence TralCS that can be spent at one time or rhe degree of change a character may bring about. They are merely an advisory measure co help Storytellers adjudicate the costs of certain ac­ tions.
Actions followed by an asterisk(*) below indicate that their effects can gener­ ally be accomplished without expending an Influence Trait.

Spectre Traits
Spectres abo have innate abilities, but ones that areclearly tainted by Oblivion.
Deatbsense - Oblivion is the only sun that Spectres know, its black hght illuminating everything they see. Spectres' accunement to Oblivion's light allows chem co gauge the strength of a wraith's Shadow. Also, living people close codeath or people who have strong Dark Passions glow with Oblivion's sickened light. Vam­ pires bum brightly with it, making them visible to spectres with their Deachsense. To sense the Oblivion in another character, a Spectre makes a Static Mental Test. If he succeeJs, he can identify the rype of bemg hefore him.
Deathsight -Spectres' Deathsight is much more intense than wraiths' since their entire being revolves around Oblivion.The Shadowlands to chem are vast plac ofdesolation, an apocalyptic landscape ravaged under Oblivion's dark oppression. Colors are faded, sounds muffleJ, the weather is always overcast, and buildings ap­ pear decaying to the point of being panially collapsed. Wraiths and other Spectres appear skeletal, diseased or decayeJ.
Dimmed Senses - Spectres lack a wraith's heightened senses. If they were hypersensitive, the Tempest's violence would overwhelm chem to the point of mak­ ing chem helpless. This reduces any perception-based tests, giving the Spectre's op­ ponent the win in case of a tie if the Spectre is in the Shadowlands. In the Tempest, a \\'rat th would beoverwhelmed by its violence and tumult, and thissituation would be reversed.

Distance - Spectres are so far removed from life, they have an increased difficult} 111 crossing the Shroud (they lose in the caseof a tie in a Static Challenge). They are unable to see any mortals when they are in the Shadowlands. They can only see wraiths and other Spectres unless they possess the Dark Arcanos Shroud­ Rending.
Hive-Mind - All spectres are connected to the Spectral Hive Mind. This means chat they have access (at v;irying levels of efficiency) to everything every Spectre has ever known. In game tenru;, this means chat Spectres can go to a Story­ teller and receive information that their characters might not have known direccly. Obviouslt, this power should be limited for player character Spectres.
Rapacity - Spectres are souls whose emotions are so raw chat any Dark Pas­ sion grabs their attention. If the negativity bemg emoted is similar to their core Bemg, they must do a Static Mental Challenge to see if they give into their dark needs with wild abandon. If they lose, they immediately attack the wraith who set them off.
